Konrad Anand, Andreas Göbel, Marcus Pappik † ​​, Will Perkins

Abstract
We provide a perfect sampling algorithm for the hard-sphere model on subsets of ℝ d \mathds{R}^{d} with expected running time linear in the volume under the assumption of strong spatial mixing. A large number of perfect and approximate sampling algorithms have been devised to sample from the hard-sphere model, and our perfect sampling algorithm is efficient for a range of parameters for which only efficient approximate samplers were previously known and is faster than these known approximate approaches. Our methods also extend to the more general setting of Gibbs point processes interacting via finite-range, repulsive potentials.



1
Introduction

Gibbs point processes, or classical gases, are mathematical models of interacting particles. In statistical physics they are used to model gases, fluids, and crystals, while in other fields they are used to model spatial phenomena such as the growth of trees in a forest, the distribution of stars in the universe, or the location of cities on a map (see e.g.
[
71
,
62
,
76
,
12
]
).

Perhaps the longest and most intensively studied Gibbs point process is the hard-sphere model: a model of a gas in which the only interaction between particles is a hard-core exclusion in a given radius around each particle. That is, it is a model of a random packing of equal-sized spheres. Despite the simplicity of its definition, the hard-sphere model is expected to exhibit the qualitative behavior of a real gas
[
2
]
, and in particular exhibits gas, liquid, and solid phases, thus giving evidence for the hypothesis, dating back to at least Boltzmann, that the macroscopic properties of a gas or fluid are determined by its microscopic interactions. This rich behavior exhibited by the hard-sphere model is very difficult to analyze rigorously, and the most fundamental questions about phase transitions in this model are open mathematical problems
[
71
,
52
]
.

In studying the hard-sphere model (or Gibbs point processes more generally), a fundamental task is to sample from the model. Sampling is used to estimate statistics, observe evidence of phase transitions, and perform statistical tests on data. A wide variety of methods have been proposed to sample from these distributions; for instance, the Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) method was first proposed by Metropolis, Rosenbluth, Rosenbluth, Teller, and Teller
[
55
]
to sample from the two-dimensional hard-sphere model. Understanding sampling methods for point processes in theory and in practice is a major area of study
[
61
,
62
,
16
,
39
,
49
]
, and advances in sampling techniques have led to advances in the understanding of the physics of these models
[
55
,
2
,
52
,
6
,
5
,
16
]
.

In this paper we will be concerned with provably efficient sampling from the hard-sphere model. Rigorous guarantees for sampling algorithms come in several different varieties. One question is what notion of ‘efficient’ to use; another is what guarantee we insist on for the output. In this paper we will provide an efficient sampling algorithm under the strictest possible terms with respect to both running time and accuracy of the output: a linear-time,
perfect
sampling algorithm.

For simplicity we focus on sampling from the hard-sphere model defined on finite boxes in
\mathds{R}^{d}
. For fixed parameter values of the model, the typical number of points appearing in such a region is linear in the volume, and so any sampling algorithm will require at least this much time.

As for guarantees on the output, there are two main types of guarantees. The first type is an
approximate sampler
: the output of such an algorithm must be distributed within
\varepsilon
total variation distance of the desired target distribution. Perhaps the main approach to efficient sampling from distributions normalized by intractable normalizing constants is the MCMC method. In this approach, one devises a Markov chain with the target distribution as the stationary distribution and runs a given number steps of the chain from a chosen starting configuration; if the number of steps is at least the
\varepsilon
-mixing time, then the final state has distribution within
\varepsilon
total variation distance of the target
[
42
,
68
,
13
]
. In general, however, computing or bounding the mixing time can be a very challenging problem.

The second type of guarantee is that of a
perfect sampler

[
66
]
. Such an algorithm has a running time that is random, but the distribution of the output is guaranteed to be
exactly
that of the target distribution. The main advantage of perfect sampling algorithms – and the primary reason they are studied and used in practice – is that one need not prove a theorem or understand the mixing time of a Markov chain to run the algorithm and get an accurate sample; one can simply run the algorithm and know that the output has the correct distribution. The drawback is that the running time may be very large, depending on the specific algorithm and on the parameter regime. Some naive sampling methods such as rejection sampling return perfect samples but are inefficient on large instances (exponential expected running time in the volume). The breakthrough of Propp and Wilson in introducing ‘coupling from the past’
[
66
,
67
]
was to devise a procedure for using a Markov chain transition matrix to design perfect sampling algorithms which, under some conditions, could run in time polylogarithmic in the size of a discrete state space (polynomial-time in the size of the graph of a spin system), matching the efficiency of fast mixing Markov chains which only return approximate samples
(see also
[
4
,
51
]
for precedents in perfect sampling). The work of Propp and Wilson led to numerous constructions of perfect sampling algorithms for problems with both discrete and continuous state spaces including
[
17
,
27
,
45
,
63
,
28
,
21
,
46
,
61
,
23
]
. Notably, many of the first applications of Propp and Wilson’s technique were in designing perfect sampling algorithms for Gibbs point processes (though often without rigorous guarantees on the efficiency of the algorithms).

Perfect sampling continues to be a very active area of research today, with a special focus on improving the range of parameters for which perfect sampling algorithms can (provably) run in expected linear or polynomial time
[
8
,
40
,
30
]

In this paper we design a perfect sampling algorithm for the hard-sphere model (and Gibbs point processes interacting with a finite-range, repulsive pair potential more generally) that is guaranteed to run in linear expected time for activity parameters up to the best known bound for efficient approximate sampling via MCMC.

What is this bound and how do we design the algorithm? One central theme in the analysis of discrete spin systems is the relationship between spatial mixing (correlation decay properties) and temporal mixing (mixing times of Markov chains)
[
35
,
1
,
75
,
54
,
15
]
. At a high level, these works show that for discrete lattice systems a strong correlation decay property (
strong spatial mixing
) implies a near-optimal convergence rate for local-update Markov chains like the Glauber dynamics. Recently it has been showed that strong spatial mixing in a discrete lattice model also implies the existence of efficient
perfect
sampling algorithms
[
18
,
3
]
. In parallel, there has been work establishing the connection between strong spatial mixing and optimal temporal mixing for Markov chains in the setting of the hard-sphere model and Gibbs point processes
[
33
,
57
,
58
]
. At a high level, our aim is to combine these threads to show that strong spatial mixing for Gibbs point processes implies the existence of an efficient perfect sampler. One challenge is that the approaches of
[
18
,
3
]
are inherently discrete in that key steps of the algorithms involve enumerating over all possible configurations in a subregion, something that is not possible in the continuum. To overcome this we make essential use of Bernoulli factories – a method for perfect simulation of a coin flip with a bias
f(p)
given access to coin flips of bias
p
. Bernoulli factories have recently been used in perfect sampling algorithms for solutions to constraint satisfaction problems in
[
31
,
32
]
.

1.1
The hard-sphere model, strong spatial mixing, and perfect sampling

The hard-sphere model is defined on a bounded, measurable subset
\Lambda
of
\mathds{R}^{d}
with an activity parameter
\lambda\geq 0
that governs the density of the model and a parameter
r>0
that governs the range of interaction (though by re-scaling there is really only one meaningful parameter, and we could take
r=1
without loss of generality). In words, the hard-sphere model is the distribution of finite point sets in
\Lambda
obtained by taking a Poisson point process of activity
\lambda
on
\Lambda
and conditioning on the event that all pairs of points are at distance at least
r
from each other; in other words, on the event that spheres of radius
r/2
centered at the given points form a sphere packing.

We can equivalently define the model more explicitly, and in doing so, introduce objects and notation we work with throughout the paper.
Fix the number of dimensions
d\in\mathds{N}
, and denote by
\mathcal{B}
the Borel
\sigma
-field on
\mathds{R}^{d}
and by
\mathcal{B}_{b}
all bounded sets in
\mathcal{B}
.
A point process on
\mathds{R}^{d}
is a probability measure on the set of locally finite point sets
\mathcal{N}=\{\eta\subset\mathds{R}^{d}\mid\forall\Lambda\in\mathcal{B}_{b}:\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta\cap\Lambda}}\right|<\infty\}
, equipped with the
\sigma
-field
\mathfrak{R}
that is generated by the maps
\big\{\mathcal{N}\to\mathds{N}_{0},\eta\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta\cap\Lambda}}\right|\ \big|\ \vphantom{\mathcal{N}\to\mathds{N}_{0},\eta\mapsto\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta\cap\Lambda}}\right|}\Lambda\in\mathcal{B}\big\}
.
The idea behind modeling gases via point processes is to represent them as random point configurations
\eta\in\mathcal{N}
, where each point
x\in\eta
indicates the (random) location of a particle.

Throughout this paper, we focus on gases that are confined in a bounded region of space.
To this end, for
\Lambda\in\mathcal{B}_{b}
, we write
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
for the set of point configurations
\eta\in\mathcal{N}
with
\eta\cap\Lambda^{c}=\emptyset
, and we write
\mathfrak{R}_{\Lambda}
for the trace of
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
in
\mathfrak{R}
.
Note that every configuration in
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
contains only finitely many points.
The hard-sphere model (or in fact any Gibbs point process) on a bounded region
\Lambda\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
is a point process
\mu_{\lambda,\Lambda}
that is only supported on
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
.

Define for every
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in\mathds{R}^{d}
the indicator that the points are centers of non-overlapping spheres of radius
r/2
; that is,

\displaystyle D\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{i,j}}\right\}\in\binom{[k]}{2}}\mathds{1}_{\mathrm{\mathrm{dist}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i},x_{j}}}\right)\geq r}\,.

Then define the hard-sphere partition function on
\Lambda\in\mathcal{B}_{b}
at activity
\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
as

Z_{\Lambda}(\lambda)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{\lambda^{k}}{k!}\int_{\Lambda^{k}}D\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}}\right)\,\text{d}x_{1}\dots\,\text{d}x_{k}\,.

For an event
A\in\mathfrak{R}
, the hard-sphere model on
\Lambda
with activity
\lambda
assigns the probability

\mu_{\lambda,\Lambda}(A)=\frac{1}{Z_{\Lambda}(\lambda)}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{\lambda^{k}}{k!}\int_{\Lambda^{k}}\mathds{1}_{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}}\right\}\in A}D\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}}\right)\,\text{d}x_{1}\dots\,\text{d}x_{k}\,.

(1)

A very useful generalization of this model is to allow for bounded, measurable activity functions
\boldsymbol{\lambda}:\mathds{R}^{d}\to\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
instead just constant activities.
Here the model is a Poisson process with inhomogenous activity
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
conditioned on the points forming the centers of a sphere packing; the partition function is now

Z_{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{k!}\int_{\Lambda^{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\boldsymbol{\lambda}(x_{i})D\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}}\right)\,\text{d}x_{1}\dots\,\text{d}x_{k}

and the measure
\mu_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\Lambda}
is defined analogously to (
1
).
This generalization allows modeling of inhomogenous spaces and generalizes the concept of imposing boundary conditions on the model (i.e., the effect of placing particles at fixed locations in space).
To see the latter, suppose we fix a point configuration
\eta\in\mathcal{N}
as boundary condition, meaning that no points are allowed to be placed in a ball of radius
r
around the points in
\eta
, then we can simply model this by setting
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(x)=0
for every point
x\in\Lambda
such that
\mathrm{\mathrm{dist}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)<r
for any
y\in\eta
.
We defer a detailed discussion to the more general setting of repulsive point processes,
and proceed by using activity functions to define
strong spatial mixing
, the condition under which we can guarantee the efficiency of our perfect sampling algorithm.

To define the concept of strong spatial mixing, we write
\pi_{\Lambda}:\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{N}
for the projection
\eta\mapsto\eta\cap\Lambda
to some region
\Lambda\in\mathcal{B}
.
Moreover, for any two point processes
P,Q
on
\mathds{R}^{d}
, we write
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P-Q}}\right\rvert_{TV}
for their total variation distance, and we write
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P-Q}}\right\rvert_{\Lambda}\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert P\circ\pi_{\Lambda}^{-1}-Q\circ\pi_{\Lambda}^{-1}}}\right\rvert_{TV}
for the total variation distance between the projections of
P
and
Q
to
\Lambda
(i.e., of their pushforward measures under
\pi_{\Lambda}
).

Strong spatial mixing asserts that, for any bounded region
\Lambda\in\mathcal{B}_{b}
and any suitable pair of activity functions
\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime}
, the distributions
\mu_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\Lambda}
and
\mu_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime},\Lambda}
are similar on any region
\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{b}
such that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime}
only differ far away from
\Lambda^{\prime}
; i.e.,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\mu_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\Lambda}-\mu_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime},\Lambda}}}\right\rvert_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
vanishes as
\mathrm{\mathrm{dist}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\Lambda^{\prime}},\mathrm{supp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\lambda}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime}}}\right)}}\right)
increases.
Writing for the volume of
{\Lambda^{\prime}}
, strong spatial mixing with exponential decay is defined as follows.

Definition 1.1
.

Given
a,b\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, the hard-sphere model on
\mathds{R}^{d}
exhibits
(a,b)
-strong spatial mixing
up to activity
\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
if for all bounded regions
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{b}
and all activity functions
\boldsymbol{\lambda},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime}\leq\lambda
it holds that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\mu_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\Lambda}-\mu_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime},\Lambda}}}\right\rvert_{\Lambda^{\prime}}\leq a\mathrm{e}^{-b\cdot\mathrm{\mathrm{dist}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\Lambda^{\prime}},\mathrm{supp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{\lambda}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\prime}}}\right)}}\right)}.

This definition of strong spatial mixing comes from
[
58
]
, which in turn adapted similar notions from discrete spin systems
[
15
,
77
]
. Strong spatial mixing has proved to be an essential definition in the analysis, both probabilistic and algorithmic, of spin systems on graphs, and many recent works are focused on either proving strong spatial mixing for a particular model, range of parameters, and class of graphs (e.g.
[
77
,
22
,
53
,
72
,
69
,
9
]
) or deriving consequences of strong spatial mixing (e.g.
[
73
,
19
,
50
,
18
,
3
]
).

Our main result is a linear expected-time perfect sampling algorithm for the hard-sphere model under the assumption of strong spatial mixing.

Theorem 1.2
.

There is a perfect sampling algorithm for the hard-sphere model on finite boxes
\Lambda\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
with the property that if the hard-sphere model exhibits
(a,b)
-strong spatial mixing up to
\lambda
, then the expected running time of the algorithm at activity
\lambda
is
O(|\Lambda|)
, where the implied constant is a function of
a,b
, and
\lambda
.

In particular, one can run the algorithm for any value of
\lambda
(without knowing whether or not strong spatial mixing holds) and the algorithm will terminate in finite time with an output distributed exactly as
\mu_{\lambda,\Lambda}
; under the assumption of strong spatial mixing the expected running time is guaranteed to be linear in the volume.

Using bounds from
[
58
]
on strong spatial mixing in the hard-sphere model, we obtain the following explicit bounds on the activities for which the algorithm is efficient.

Corollary 1.3
.

The above perfect sampling algorithm runs in expected time
O(|\Lambda|)
when
\lambda<\frac{\mathrm{e}}{v_{d}(r)}
, where
v_{d}(r)
is the volume of the ball of radius
r
in
\mathds{R}^{d}
.

In comparison, near-linear time MCMC-based approximate samplers were given in
[
58
]
for the same range of parameters (following results for more restricted ranges in
[
43
,
33
]
). For perfect sampling from the hard-sphere model, linear expected time algorithms were given in
[
36
,
25
]
for more restrictive ranges of parameters.

1.2
Gibbs point processes with finite-range repulsive potentials

We now give a closely related result in the more general setting of Gibbs point processes interacting via finite-range, repulsive pair potentials.

Gibbs point processes are defined via a density against an underlying Poisson point process. In general, this density is the exponential of (the negative of) an energy function on point sets that captures the interactions between points. In many of the most studied cases, this energy function takes a special form: it is the sum of potentials over pairs of points in a configuration.

A
pair potential
is a measurable symmetric function
\phi:\mathds{R}^{d}\times\mathds{R}^{d}\to\mathds{R}\cup\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\infty}}\right\}
. For a bounded, measurable activity function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
on
\Lambda
the Gibbs point process with pair potential
\phi
on
\Lambda
is defined via the partition function

Z_{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{k!}\int_{\Lambda^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-H\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}}\right)}\,\text{d}x_{1}\dots\,\text{d}x_{k}

where

H\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}}\right)=\sum_{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{i,j}}\right\}\in\binom{[k]}{2}}\phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i},x_{j}}}\right)\,,

denotes the Hamiltonian given by the potential
\phi
.
Again the corresponding probability measure
\mu_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\Lambda}
is obtained as in (
1
).
Note that the hard-sphere model discussed earlier is obtained by setting
\phi(x,y)=\infty
if
\mathrm{\mathrm{dist}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)<r
and
\phi(x,y)=0
otherwise.
Making sure that the potential in question is always clear from the context, we allow ourselves to omit the dependency on the potential from the notation.
For the same reason, we use the same notation for the general model and the hard-sphere model.

A pair potential
\phi
is
repulsive
if
\phi(x,y)\geq 0
for all
x,y
.
It is of
finite-range
if there exists
r\geq 0
so that
\phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)=0
whenever
\mathrm{\mathrm{dist}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)\geq r
.
Typical example of models interacting via a finite-range, repulsive pair potential are the hard-sphere model (as illustrated above) or the Strauss process
[
74
,
44
]
.

As with the hard-sphere model, we can use the activity function to encode the influence of boundary conditions.
To this end, given a repulsive potential
\phi
and an activity function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
, we account for the impact of a boundary condition
\eta\in\mathcal{N}
by defining the modified activity function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\eta}:y\mapsto\boldsymbol{\lambda}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\sum_{x\in\eta}\phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)}
.
We then define the partition function and Gibbs point process on
\Lambda\in\mathcal{B}_{b}
with activity function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
and boundary condition
\eta
by
Z_{\Lambda}^{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\coloneqq Z_{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\eta})
and
\mu_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\Lambda}^{\eta}\coloneqq\mu_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\eta},\Lambda}
.
In the case of constant activity functions
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\equiv\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
, our notation simplifies to
Z_{\Lambda}^{\eta}(\lambda)
and
\mu_{\lambda,\Lambda}^{\eta}
respectively.

We proceed by defining strong spatial mixing for a Gibbs point process exactly as in Definition
1.1
.
Our next result is a near-linear expected time perfect sampling algorithm for Gibbs point processes interacting via finite-range, repulsive potentials under the assumption of strong spatial mixing.

Theorem 1.4
.

Suppose
\phi
is a finite-range, repulsive potential on
\mathds{R}^{d}
and suppose
\phi
exhibits
(a,b)
-strong spatial mixing up to
\lambda
for some constants
a,b>0
. Then there is a perfect sampling algorithm for the Gibbs point process defined by
\phi
and activity bounded by
\lambda
on boxes
\Lambda
in
\mathds{R}^{d}
with expected running time
O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log^{O(1)}}}\right)
.

One difference between this algorithm and the hard-sphere algorithm of Theorem
1.2
is that this algorithm needs knowledge of the constants
a,b
in the assumption of strong spatial mixing, whereas the hard-sphere algorithm does not.

Using the results of
[
58
]
, we can get explicit bounds for the existence of efficient perfect sampling algorithms in terms of the
temperedness constant
of the potential defined by

C_{\phi}:=\sup_{x\in\mathds{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathds{R}^{d}}|1-e^{-\phi(x,y)}|\,dy\,.

(2)

Under the assumption that
\phi
is repulsive and of finite range
r
, we have
0\leq C_{\phi}\leq v_{d}(r)
.

Corollary 1.5
.

The above perfect sampling algorithm runs in expected time
O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log^{O(1)}}}\right)
when
\lambda<\frac{\mathrm{e}}{C_{\phi}}
.

Remark 1.6
.

In fact, using the results of Michelen and Perkins
[
56
]
, one can push the bound for strong spatial mixing up to
e/\Delta_{\phi}
, where
\Delta_{\phi}\leq C_{\phi}
is the potential-weighted connective constant defined therein; our perfect sampling algorithm is efficient up to that point.

1.3
Related work and future directions

Related work

In recent years there has been a moderate flurry of activity around proving rigorous results for Gibbs point processes in both the setting of statistical physics and probability theory and in the setting of provably efficient sampling algorithms.

Work on provably efficient approximate sampling methods for the hard-sphere model begins with the seminal paper of Kannan, Mahoney, and Montenegro
[
43
]
, who used techniques from the analysis of discrete spin systems to prove mixing time bounds for Markov chains for the hard-sphere model. Improvements to the range of parameters for which fast mixing holds came in
[
29
,
33
]
, before Michelen and Perkins proved the bound
e/v_{d}(r)
in
[
58
]
, which we match with a perfect sampling algorithm in Corollary
1.3
.

Perfect sampling algorithms for the hard sphere model have been considered in
[
27
,
46
,
21
,
25
,
38
]
. In terms of rigorous guarantees of efficiency, Huber proved a bound of
2/v_{d}(r)
for a near-linear expected time perfect sampler in
[
36
]
. The perfect sampling algorithm of Guo and Jerrum in
[
25
]
does not match this bound, but the algorithm, based on ‘partial rejection sampling’
[
26
]
is novel and particularly simple. Several of these approaches also apply for finite-range, repulsive potentials or can be extended to that setting (e.g.
[
60
]
).

In parallel, there has been much work on proving bounds on the range of activities for which no phase transition can occur in the hard-sphere model; and, in recent years in particular, the techniques used have close connections to algorithms and the study of Markov chains. The classic approach to proving absence of phase transition is by proving convergence of the cluster expansion; the original bound here is
1/(ev_{d}(r))
due to Groeneveld
[
24
]
. In small dimensions (most significantly in dimension
2
) improvements to the radius of convergence can be obtained
[
20
]
. On the other hand, this approach is inherently limited by the presence of non-physical singularities on the negative real axis. Alternative approaches avoiding this obstruction include using the equivalence of spatial and temporal mixing
[
33
,
58
]
; or disagreement percolation
[
10
,
34
,
7
]
. The best current bound for absence of phase transition for the hard-sphere model and for repulsive pair potentials is the bound of
e/C_{\phi}
(and
e/\Delta_{\phi}
) obtained by Michelen and Perkins
[
57
,
58
,
56
]
. Theorem
1.4
brings the bound for efficient perfect sampling up to this bound.

On a technical level, the most relevant past work is
[
18
]
, in which the authors prove that for discrete spin systems, strong spatial mixing and subexponential volume growth of a sequence of graphs imply the existence of an efficient perfect sampling algorithm. We take their approach as a starting point but need new ideas to replace their exhaustive enumeration of configurations.

A key step in our algorithm is the use of a Bernoulli factory to implement a Bayes filter. Bernoulli factories are algorithms by which a Bernoulli random variable with success probability
f(p)
can be simulated (perfectly) by an algorithm with access to independent Bernoulli
p
random variables, where the algorithm does not know the value
p
. Whether a Bernoulli factory exists (and how efficient it can be) depends on the function
f(\cdot)
and a priori bounds on the possible values
p
. Bernoulli factories have been studied in
[
64
,
37
,
14
]
and recently used in the design of perfect sampling algorithms for CSP solutions in
[
31
,
32
]
.

Future directions

There are a number of extensions and improvements to these results one could pursue.
Perhaps most straightforward would be to relax the notion of strong spatial mixing from exponential decay to decay faster than the volume growth of
\mathds{R}^{d}
and to extend the results to repulsive potentials of unbounded range but finite temperedness constant
C_{\phi}
. Moreover, it would be nice to upgrade the guarantees of the algorithm in Theorem
1.4
to that of Theorem
1.2
: that the algorithm does not need prior knowledge of the strong spatial mixing constants
a,b
to run correctly.

An ambitious and exciting direction would be to remove the assumption of a repulsive potential and find efficient perfect sampling algorithms for the class of
stable
potentials (see e.g.
[
65
,
70
,
71
]
for a definition). A stable potential is repulsive at short ranges but can include a weak attractive part; such potentials include the physically realistic Lenard-Jones potential among others
[
78
]
.
This would require some very new ideas, as much of the recent probabilistic and algorithmic work on Gibbs point processes (e.g.
[
57
,
58
,
7
,
56
]
) has used repulsiveness as an essential ingredient (for one, repulsiveness of the potential implies stochastic domination by the underlying Poisson point process).
As a notable exception, a deterministic approximation algorithm for partition functions of finite-range stable potentials based on cluster expansion was recently proposed in
[
41
]
.

1.4
Outline of the paper

In
Section

2
, we describe the high-level idea and intuition behind the algorithm. In
Section

3
we introduce some notation and present some preliminary results that we will use throughout the paper. In
Section

4
we present the algorithm that we will apply to both hard spheres and more general processes. In
Section

5
we prove correctness of the algorithm. In
Section

6
we prove a technical lemma that will be crucial for showing efficiency of our algorithm under the assumption of strong spatial mixing. In
Section

7
we specialize to the hard-sphere model to complete the proof of
Theorem

1.2
. In
Section

8
we work with finite-range, repulsive potentials to complete the proof of
Theorem

1.4
. In
Section

9
we prove the running time bound for the Bernoulli factory used by our algorithm. The appendix contains some technical lemmas on measure theory and stochastic processes.

2
Intuitive idea behind the algorithm

Our algorithm is an adaptation of the work by Feng, Guo, and Yin
[
18
]
on perfect sampling from discrete spin systems to continuum models.
We mimic their setting of a spin system on a graph
G=(V,E)
by considering a graphical structure on sub-regions of our continuous space.

Let
\Lambda=[0,L)^{d}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
be the region considered,
\lambda>0
the activity, and let
\phi
be a repulsive potential of range
r>0
.
The main idea is to subdivide
\Lambda
into boxes of (roughly) side length
r
, indexed by
\mathcal{V}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0,\dots,N-1}}\right\}^{d}
for
N=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil L/r}}\right\rceil
.
Each box index
\boldsymbol{v}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{d})\in\mathcal{V}
is associated with the sub-region

\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}=\big([v_{1}r,(v_{1}+1)r)\times\dots\times[v_{d}r,(v_{d}+1)r)\big)\cap\Lambda.

We extend this notation to sets of box indices
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
by setting
\Lambda_{S}=\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{v}\in S}\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}
.
Further, for
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{V}
, we write
\mathds{B}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{v}}}\right)
for the set of boxes
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathcal{V}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq k
, and we denote by
\partial S=(\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{v}\in S}\mathds{B}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{v}}}\right))\setminus S
for the outer boundary of
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
.
For readers familiar with the work of Feng, Guo, and Yin
[
18
]
on discrete spin systems, it will be helpful to think of
\mathcal{V}
as the vertices of a graph, where two vertices
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathcal{V}
are adjacent if
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}}\right\rVert_{\infty}=1
.

Often, it will be convenient to not differentiate between a set of box indices
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
and the associated region
\Lambda_{S}
.
More precisely, we write
\mathcal{N}_{S}
for the point sets in
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{S}}
, we denote by
Z_{S}(\lambda)
and
\mu_{\lambda,S}
the partition function and the Gibbs point process on
\Lambda_{S}
, and, for a point set
\eta\in\mathcal{N}
, we write
\eta\cap S
and
\eta\setminus S
for
\eta\cap\Lambda_{S}
and
\eta\setminus\Lambda_{S}
.
A notable exception from this abuse of notation is that we always write
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|S}}\right|
for the cardinality of the set
S
and for the total volume of the boxes indicated by
S
.
Moreover, the set complement
S^{c}
should be understood as
\mathcal{V}\setminus S
, which is then associated with the region
\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{S}
(opposed to
\mathds{R}^{d}\setminus\Lambda_{S}
).

Our algorithm runs iteratively, keeping track of two random variables: a point configuration
X_{t}\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
with
X_{0}=\emptyset,
and a set of ‘incorrect’ boxes
\mathcal{U}_{t}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
with
\mathcal{U}_{0}=\mathcal{V}
.
With each iteration
t
we maintain the following
invariant
: the partial configuration
X_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}_{t}^{c}
is distributed according to the projection of
\mu_{\lambda,\mathcal{U}_{t}^{c}}^{X_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}_{t}}
(i.e., the Gibbs point process on
\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{\mathcal{U}_{t}}
with boundary condition
X_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}_{t}
).
It follows that
X_{t}
is distributed according to
\mu_{\lambda,\Lambda}
once we reach the state
\mathcal{U}_{t}=\emptyset
.

In every iteration, the algorithm tries to update the point configuration
X_{t}
on a subset of boxes
B\subseteq\mathcal{V}
.
To this end, given an
update radius

\ell\in\mathds{N}
, we define for every
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
and
\boldsymbol{v}\in S
the set of box indices

\displaystyle B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\ell}}\right)\coloneqq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}_{\ell}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{v}}}\right)\setminus S}}\right)\cup\boldsymbol{v}.

(3)

We proceed by sketching an iteration of the algorithm.
An example for the involved subregions is given in
Figure

1
.
Each iteration runs as follows:

1.

We choose
\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\in\mathcal{U}_{t}
uniformly at random and attempt to ‘repair’ it by updating
X_{t}
on a neighborhood of boxes
B=B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{t},\ell}}\right)
as given in (
3
).

2.

We sample a Bayes filter
F_{t}
(i.e., a Bernoulli random variable) with probability depending on the potential
\phi
, the activity
\lambda
, and the current point configuration
X_{t}
on
\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{u}_{t}}
and
\Lambda_{\partial B}
.

3.

(a)

If
F_{t}=1
, we set
\mathcal{U}_{t+1}=\mathcal{U}_{t}\setminus\boldsymbol{u}_{t}
, and we obtain
X_{t+1}
by updating
X_{t}
on
\Lambda_{B}
according to a sample from
\mu_{\lambda,B}^{X_{t}\cap B^{c}}
(i.e., the Gibbs point process on
\Lambda_{B}
with boundary condition
X_{t}\cap B^{c}
).

(b)

If
F_{t}=0
, the configuration is unchanged and we add the boundary boxes to our ‘incorrect’ list, i.e.,
X_{t+1}=X_{t}
and
\mathcal{U}_{t+1}=\mathcal{U}_{t}\cup\partial B
.

Figure 1
:
The box-shaped region
\Lambda\subset\mathds{R}^{2}
is divided into boxes of side length
r
(dotted lines). The boxes
\mathcal{U}_{t}
are bordered by bold black lines. For
\boldsymbol{u}_{t}
as given and update radius
\ell=2
, the corresponding set
B
of boxes to be updated is indicated by the red hatched area (falling left to right). Its boundary boxes
\partial B
are shown as blue hatched area (rising left to right). The boxes in
H=(\mathcal{U}_{t}\cup B)^{\mathrm{c}}
are shown with gray background.

We use the Bayes filter, as in
[
18
]
, to remove bias from the resulting distribution. To give some intuition for its role, suppose we run a naive version of the algorithm where we always update
X_{t}
on
\Lambda_{B}
as in step 3.a) above.
Assuming the desired invariant holds after
t
iterations, this naive algorithm gives a bias to the distribution of
X_{t+1}
proportional to
\frac{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{u}_{t}}^{X_{t}\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{u}_{t})}(\lambda)}{Z_{B}^{X_{t}\cap\partial B}(\lambda)}
.
We choose the Bayes filter such that, conditioned on
F_{t}=1
, the bias term gets canceled.
This suggests the choice

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{F_{t}=1}X_{t},\mathcal{U}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{t}}}\right.}}}\right]=C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{t},X_{t}}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{X_{t}\cap\partial B}(\lambda)}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{u}_{t}}^{X_{t}\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{u}_{t})}(\lambda)},

(4)

where scaling
C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{t},X_{t}}}\right)
serves three main purposes.

First, it must guarantee that the right-hand side of (
4
) is a probability.
To achieve this we need, for
H=(\mathcal{U}_{t}\cup B)^{\mathrm{c}}
and almost all realizations of
X_{t}
,
\mathcal{U}_{t}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{t}
, that

\displaystyle C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{t},X_{t}}}\right)\leq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\mathcal{N}_{H}\end{subarray}}\frac{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{u}_{t}}^{\xi\cup(X_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}_{t})}(\lambda)}{Z_{B}^{\xi\cup(X_{t}\cap(\mathcal{U}_{t}\setminus\boldsymbol{u}_{t}))}(\lambda)}.

(5)

Second,
C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{t},X_{t}}}\right)
must introduce no new bias.
Carrying out the necessary calculations, it can be shown that this is guaranteed if
C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{t},X_{t}}}\right)
only depends on
X_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}_{t}
.
Finally, it must ensure that the algorithm terminates almost surely.
It suffices to ensure
C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{t},X_{t}}}\right)
is uniformly bounded away from
0
for almost all realizations of
X_{t}
,
implying that the same holds for the right-hand side of (
4
).
We refer to a function
C(\cdot)
satisfying these requirements as a
Bayes filter correction
.

If we use a Bayes filter as given in (
4
), keeping
X_{t}
and
\mathcal{U}_{t}
unchanged whenever
F_{t}=0
introduces new bias.
To prevent this, we set
\mathcal{U}_{t+1}=\mathcal{U}_{t}\cup\partial B
in step 3.b), effectively deleting the part of the configuration that was revealed by the filter.
Since the algorithm only terminates once
\mathcal{U}_{t}=\emptyset
, we further require the Bayes filter correction to ensure that the probability of
F_{t}=0
is small to guarantee efficiency.

Constructing a Bayes filter correction that satisfies the requirements above and allows for efficient sampling of
F_{t}
is a non-trivial task.
In the next subsections, we present two approaches for this, the first specialized to the hard-sphere model without requirements, and the second one for more general potentials under the assumption of strong spatial mixing.
Crucially, assuming strong spatial mixing, both constructions allow us to control the success probability of the Bayes filter via the update radius
\ell
in the construction of the updated set of boxes
B
(see step 1 and (
3
)).

2.1
Bayes filter for the hard-sphere model

To construct a Bayes filter for the hard-sphere model, we efficiently approximate the right-hand side of (
5
).
To approximate the infimum over the uncountable set of configurations
\xi\in\mathcal{N}_{H}
we take the minimum over a finite, but sufficiently rich set of configurations, balancing the quality of approximation with the computation required. In fact the number of configurations needed will depend only on the volume of
\Lambda_{B\cup\partial B}
.
We approximate the fraction of partition functions in (
5
) with running time only depending on the volume of
\Lambda_{B\cup\partial B}
.
As a result, we efficiently compute a Bayes filter correction
C_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
, with the parameter
\varepsilon>0
controlling how much
C_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{t},X_{t}}}\right)
deviates from the right-hand side of (
5
).

While our construction of
C_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
guarantees correctness of the sampling algorithm for any
\varepsilon>0
, proving efficiency requires additional assumptions.
To this end, we show that strong spatial mixing allows us to choose
\varepsilon
so that the probability that
F_{t}=0
is uniformly bounded above, ensuring
\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)
iterations of the algorithm in expectation.

It remains to argue that we can efficiently sample
F_{t}
, using the Bayes filter correction
C_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
.
Explicitly computing the success probability of
F_{t}
as in (
4
) would require computing the fraction of partition functions on the right-hand side exactly, while approximating these partition functions would require that the approximation error only depends on
X_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}_{t}
, to avoid new bias.

It is unclear how to implement these approaches, so instead we use Bernoulli factories to sample
F_{t}
without knowing the success probability.
To do so, we observe that the fraction of partition functions can be written as a ratio of probabilities for drawing the empty set from a conditional hard-sphere model on
\Lambda_{B}
and
\Lambda_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{u}_{t}}
.
Since both regions have constant volume, rejection sampling gives Bernoulli random variables with these success probabilities in constant time.
Hence, we obtain a Bernoulli factory for
F_{t}
with constant expected running time.
Wald’s identity yields a total expected running time
\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)
for the algorithm.

2.2
Bayes filter for general potentials

We now consider the case of general bounded-range, repulsive potentials.
Unlike the hard sphere model, it is not clear here how to approximate the infimum in (
5
) from a finite set of boundary configurations.
However, given constants
a,b>0
such that
\phi
satisfies
(a,b)
-strong spatial mixing, we can explicitly compute a function
\delta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a,b}}\right)
so that

C_{a,b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{t},X_{t}}}\right)=\delta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a,b}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{u}_{t}}^{X_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}_{t}}(\lambda)}{Z_{B}^{X_{t}\cap(\mathcal{U}_{t}\setminus\boldsymbol{u}_{t})}(\lambda)}

is a Bayes filter correction.
With strong spatial mixing, we use
C_{a,b}(\cdot)
to construct a Bayes filter such that probability that
F_{t}=0
is bounded above, again implying a bound of
\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)
on the expected number of iterations of the algorithm.

Note that in this setting, we require spatial mixing for both correctness and efficiency, while for the hard-sphere model we only need it for efficiency.
Another crucial difference is that, while we can explicitly compute
\delta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a,b}}\right)
, the same does not hold for
C_{a,b}(\cdot)
due to the fraction of partition functions involved.
Again we circumvent this by rewriting the success probability of the Bayes filter in a suitable way and applying a Bernoulli factory for sampling
F_{t}
.
Finally, we point out that we do not obtain a constant bound for the expected running time of each iteration, but instead the bound depends on the number of points in
X_{t}\cap\partial B
.
Possible dependencies between the configuration
X_{t}
and the number of iterations prevent us from bounding the total expected running time using Wald’s identity.
Instead, we provide tail bounds on the number of iterations and the running time of each iteration, allowing us to derive an expected total running time that is linear in the volume of
\Lambda
up to polylogarithmic factors.

3
Preliminaries

Throughout the paper, we write
\mathds{N}
for the set of strictly positive integers, and we write
\mathds{N}_{0}=\mathds{N}\cup\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0}}\right\}
.
For any
k\in\mathds{N}
, we denote by
[k]
the set
[1,k]\cap\mathds{N}
.

For a point configuration
\eta\in\mathcal{N}
, we write
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta}}\right|\in\mathds{N}_{0}\cup\{\infty\}
for the number of points in
\eta
.
Note that this notation is the same that as the one we use for the volume of a region. The particular meaning will be clear from the context.
Moreover, for
k\in\mathds{N}
, we write
\binom{\eta}{k}
for the set
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\eta^{\prime}\subseteq\eta\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\eta^{\prime}\subseteq\eta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta^{\prime}}}\right|=k}}\right.}}\right\}
.

3.1
Gibbs point processes

Throughout the paper, we use the definitions and notation for point sets and Gibbs point processes introduced in
Sections

1.1
and
1.2
.
However, we will allow ourselves some notational shortcuts.
Firstly, when dealing with a tuple
(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in(\mathds{R}^{d})^{k}
we frequently denote it by the corresponding bold letter
\boldsymbol{x}
.
Based on this, we write
\,\text{d}\boldsymbol{x}
for
\,\text{d}x_{1}\dots\,\text{d}x_{k}
and
H\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{x}}}\right)
for
H\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}}\right)
.
Moreover, for any
k\in\mathds{N}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in(\mathds{R}^{d})^{k}
we write
\eta_{\boldsymbol{x}}
for the set
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}}\right\}
, where the case
k=0
results in
\eta_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\emptyset
.
Finally, for
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Lambda^{k}
we write
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\boldsymbol{x}}
for
\prod_{i\in[k]}\boldsymbol{\lambda}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)
.
This allows us to write the partition function on
\Lambda\in\mathcal{B}_{b}
with activity function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
as

Z_{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{k!}\int_{\Lambda^{k}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\boldsymbol{x}}\mathrm{e}^{-H\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{x}}}\right)}\,\text{d}\boldsymbol{x}

and the probability of
A\in\mathfrak{R}
under
\mu_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\Lambda}
as

\mu_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\Lambda}(A)=\frac{1}{Z_{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{k!}\int_{\Lambda^{k}}\mathds{1}_{\eta_{\boldsymbol{x}}\in A}\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{\boldsymbol{x}}\mathrm{e}^{-H\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{x}}}\right)}\,\text{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

As discussed in the introduction, we express the impact of a boundary conditions
\eta\in\mathcal{N}
by considering the modified activity function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\eta}:y\mapsto\boldsymbol{\lambda}(y)\mathrm{e}^{-\sum_{x\in\eta}\phi(x,y)}
, and we write
Z_{\Lambda}^{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\coloneqq Z_{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\eta})
and
\mu_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\Lambda}^{\eta}\coloneqq\mu_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\eta},\Lambda}
for the respective partition function and Gibbs point process with boundary condition
\eta
.
Moreover, if the activity function is constant
\boldsymbol{\lambda}\equiv\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
, our notation simplifies to
Z_{\Lambda}^{\eta}(\lambda)
and
\mu_{\lambda,\Lambda}^{\eta}
respectively.
Finally,, if the
\lambda
is clear from the context, we omit it and write
Z_{\Lambda}^{\eta}
and
\mu_{\Lambda}^{\eta}
.

We introduce further concepts related to Gibbs point processes, such a point density functions when they are required.
Moreover, various useful properties of Gibbs point processes are given in
Appendix

C
.

3.2
Bernoulli factories

In designing our sampling algorithm, it will be useful to consider the following Bernoulli factory problem. We are given access to a sampler for
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
and for
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)
, that is samplers of Bernoulli random variables with parameters
p
and
q
respectively, where we further assume
p<q
. We want to sample a random variable
Z\sim\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p}{q}}}\right)
.

Most work on Bernoulli factories studies their running time in terms of the number of coin flips required. In our setting, the time needed to generate each of these coin flips is random variable. Fortunately, suitable independence assumptions hold in our setting allowing us to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

Fix some
p,q\in[0,1]
such that
q-p\geq\epsilon
for some
\epsilon>0
. Further assume that we have oracle access to a sampler from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
and
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)
in the following sense:

1.

every sample from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
(resp.
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)
) is independent from all previous samples;

2.

the expected running time for obtaining a sample from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
(resp.
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)
), conditioned on previously obtained samples, is uniformly bounded by some
t\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
.

Then we can sample from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p}{q}}}\right)
in
\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({t\epsilon^{-2}}}}\right)
expected time.

Lemma

3.1
, will play a key role in bounding the expected running time of our algorithm. To proceed with the formal description of our algorithm, we defer the proof of this lemma to
Section

9
.

4
The algorithm

Let
\Lambda=[0,L)^{d}
and consider a Gibbs point processes on
\Lambda
with uniform activity
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(x)\equiv\lambda
for some
\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{>0}

and repulsive potential
\phi
with finite range
r\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
Throughout the analysis of our algorithm, it will be useful to focus on configurations
\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
such that
\phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)<\infty
for all
{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x,y}}\right\}\in\binom{\eta}{2}}
, in which case we call
\eta
a
feasible configuration
.

We use the method of splitting
\Lambda
into smaller boxes that we introduced in
Section

2
, along with the same definitions and notation.
As discussed earlier, our algorithm runs in multiple iterations, and the update steps in every iteration
t
depends on the outcome of a Bernoulli random variable
F_{t}
, called the
Bayes filter
.
The construction of this Bayes filter is closely tied to the following definition.

Definition 4.1
.

Fix a repulsive potential
\phi
of range
r\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, an activity
\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
and some
\ell\in\mathds{N}
.
We call a function
C:2^{\mathcal{V}}\times\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{N}\to[0,1]
a
Bayes filter correction
if,
for all non-empty
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
and
\boldsymbol{v}\in S
, it holds that

1.

The map
C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\cdot}}\right)
is
\mathfrak{R}
-measurable and satisfies
C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)=C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta\cap S}}\right)
for all
\eta\in\mathcal{N}
,

2.

there is some
\varepsilon>0
such that for
B=B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\ell}}\right)
,
H=(S\cup B)^{c}
and all feasible
\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
it holds that

\varepsilon\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\leq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\mathcal{N}_{H}\\
\xi\cup(\eta\cap S)\text{ is feasible}\end{subarray}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\xi\cup(\eta\cap S)}}{Z_{B}^{\xi\cup(\eta\cap(S\setminus\boldsymbol{v}))}}}}\right\}.

Note that by 1. it holds that
C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\cdot}}\right)
is fully characterized by its behavior on
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
.

Our perfect sampling procedure is stated in
Algorithm

1
.

Data:

region
\Lambda=[0,L)^{d}
, repulsive potential
\phi
of range at most
r\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, activity
\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, update radius
\ell\in\mathds{N}

set
t=0
,
\mathcal{U}_{t}=\mathcal{V}
,
X_{t}=\emptyset

while

\mathcal{U}_{t}\neq\emptyset

do

1

draw
\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\in\mathcal{U}_{t}
uniformly at random

set
B=B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{t},\ell}}\right)
as defined in (
3
)

draw
F_{t}
from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{t},X_{t}}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{X_{t}\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{u}_{t}}^{X_{t}\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{u}_{t})}}}}\right)
where
C
is a Bayes filter correction as in
Definition

4.1

if

F_{t}
= 1

then

2

draw
Y
from
\mu_{B}^{X_{t}\cap B^{c}}

set
X_{t+1}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{t}\setminus B}}\right)\cup Y

set
\mathcal{U}_{t+1}=\mathcal{U}_{t}\setminus\boldsymbol{u}_{t}

else

3

4

set
\mathcal{U}_{t+1}=\mathcal{U}_{t}\cup\partial B

increase
t
by
1

return

X_{t}

Algorithm 1

Perfect sampling algorithm for repulsive Gibbs point processes

To analyze
Algorithm

1
, it will help to think of it as a Markov chain
(X_{t},\mathcal{U}_{t},F_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{t})_{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}}
, which we set to remain constant once it hits a state with
\mathcal{U}_{t}=\emptyset
.
We write
\Omega=(\mathcal{N}\times 2^{\mathcal{V}}\times\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0,1}}\right\}\times\mathcal{V})^{\mathds{N}_{0}}
for the state space of all trajectories of that Markov chain, which we equip the
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{A}=(\mathfrak{R}\otimes 2^{2^{\mathcal{V}}}\otimes 2^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0,1}}\right\}}\otimes 2^{\mathcal{V}})^{\otimes\mathds{N}_{0}}
, and we denote by
\mathds{P}
the distribution on
(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
induced by
Algorithm

1
.
Note that in particular
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{0}=\emptyset,\mathcal{U}_{0}=\mathcal{V}}}}\right]=1
.

Before we analyze the correctness and running time of
Algorithm

1
, we first argue that each update step is well-defined.
In particular, we need to show that the success probability of the Bayes filter that we require in line
1
is indeed a probability.
Moreover, we convince ourselves that the algorithm terminates almost surely after finitely many iterations.
For this, we use the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose
C
is a Bayes filter correction.
Let
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
be non-empty,
\boldsymbol{v}\in S
and
B=B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\ell}}\right)
.
There is some
\varepsilon>0
such that, for all feasible
\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
, it holds that

\varepsilon\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}\leq 1.

Proof.

Fix
S
and
\boldsymbol{v}
.
For the lower bound, note that for all
\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}

Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}\geq Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap\partial B}\geq Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}.

Thus, by the definition of a Bayes filter correction, there is some
\varepsilon>0
such that for all feasible
\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}

C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}\geq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\geq\varepsilon.

To derive the upper bound, note that by
Lemma

C.2

Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}=Z_{B}^{\eta\cap B^{c}}
and
Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}=Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c}}
.
Next, set
H=(S\cup B)^{c}
and note that, for feasible
\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
, it holds that
(\eta\cap H)\cup(\eta\cap S)
is feasible as well.
By the definition of a Bayes filter correction, this implies

C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\leq\frac{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c}}}{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap B^{c}}}.

Consequently, it holds that

C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}=C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap B^{c}}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c}}}\leq 1,

which proves the claim
∎

We use the previous lemma to derive the following statement, which will guarantee that
Algorithm

1
is well-defined and terminates almost surely.

Lemma 4.3
.

The following holds throughout
Algorithm

1
:

1.

For every
t\in\mathds{N}_{0}
,
X_{t}
is almost surely feasible.

2.

There is some
\varepsilon>0
such that for all
t\in\mathds{N}_{0}
it holds that almost surely
\mathcal{U}_{t}=\emptyset
or

\varepsilon\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{t},X_{t}}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{X_{t}\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{u}_{t}}^{X_{t}\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{u}_{t})}}\leq 1.

Proof.

We prove this statement via induction over the iteration
t\in\mathds{N}_{0}
.
For
t=0
, note that
X_{0}=\emptyset
.
Thus,
1
is trivially true.
Moreover,
2
follows from applying
Lemma

4.2
to
C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{V},\boldsymbol{v},\emptyset}}\right)
for every
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{V}
.

Now, suppose our claim holds at some iteration
t\in N
.
We start by showing that
1
holds in iteration
t+1
.
First, note that if
\mathcal{U}_{t}=\emptyset
, then there is nothing to prove.
Thus, we may assume
\mathcal{U}_{t}\neq\emptyset
.
If
F_{t}=0
, then
X_{t+1}=X_{t}
.
Thus, in this case,
X_{t+1}
is feasible if and only if
X_{t}
was feasible, which holds almost surely by the induction hypothesis.
Next, consider the case
F_{t}=1
and set
B=B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{t},\ell}}\right)
.
By the induction hypothesis, we have that
X_{t}\cap B^{c}\subseteq X_{t}
is almost surely feasible.
Further, note that
\mu_{B}^{X_{t}\cap B^{c}}
is only supported on
\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{B}
such that
\eta\cup(X_{t}\cap B^{c})
is feasible.
Hence, we have that
X_{t+1}=(X_{t+1}\cap B)\cup(X_{t}\cap B^{c})
is almost surely feasible, proving
1
.
For
2
, assume that
\mathcal{U}_{t+1}\neq\emptyset
.
Applying
Lemma

4.2
for every non-empty
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
and
\boldsymbol{v}\in S
yields the desired bounds on
C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t+1},\boldsymbol{u}_{t+1},X_{t+1}}}\right)
whenever
X_{t+1}
is feasible.
As we have just shown, this is the case almost surely, which concludes the proof.
∎

Considering
Algorithm

1
, an immediate question is how to construct the Bayes filter correction in line
1
, and in particular, how to do so in such a way that the Bayes filter
F_{t}
can be sampled efficiently.
However, we will defer this question for now and first prove that
Algorithm

1
produces the correct output distribution.

5
Proof of correctness

In this section we prove that
Algorithm

1
produces the correct output distribution.
That is for
T=\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}}\mathcal{U}_{t}=\emptyset}}\right.}}\right\}
it holds that
X_{T}\sim\mu_{\Lambda}
.
We first show that the number of iterations
T
is finite almost surely.
This directly follows as a corollary of
Lemma

4.3
.

Corollary 5.1
.

Algorithm

1
terminates almost surely after finitely many iterations.
That is, for
T=\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}}\mathcal{U}_{t}=\emptyset}}\right.}}\right\}
we have
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T<\infty}}}\right]=1
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

4.3
, the probability that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|
decreases in each step is uniformly bounded away from
0
.
Thus, there is a positive probability of going from any
\mathcal{U}_{t}
to the empty set in
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|
steps.
Since
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{V}}}\right|
, this means for every
k\in\mathds{N}
, it holds that the probability that
\mathcal{U}_{k\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{V}}}\right|}=\emptyset
is bounded away from
0
uniformly in
k
.
Thus,
T
is dominated by a geometric random variable with strictly positive success probability, which proves the claim.
∎

Before going into the technical part of proving correctness, a few remarks about our notation are in place.
Firstly, for any
A\in\mathcal{A}
with
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{A}}}\right]>0
we write
\mathds{P}_{A}
as shorthand for the probability measure
\mathds{P}_{A}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\,\cdot}}}\right]=\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\,\cdot\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\,\cdot}A}}\right.}}}\right]
.
Note that for all events
A,B\in\mathcal{A}
with
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{A\cap B}}}\right]>0
it holds that
\mathds{P}_{A\cap B}=(\mathds{P}_{A})_{B}
.
Throughout our proofs, we use conditional expectations to make conditioning on partial point configurations
X_{t}\cap{\Lambda^{\prime}}
rigorous.
In particular, we frequently condition on a sub-
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
and an event
A\in\mathcal{A}
with
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{A}}}\right]>0
at the same time.
Formally, for a measurable function
f:\Omega\to\mathds{R}
we write
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{f}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]
for the conditional expectation of
f
given
\mathcal{F}
under the conditional measure
\mathds{P}_{A}
.
Note that any identity involving
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{f}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]
should be understood to hold
\mathds{P}_{A}
-almost surely.
Moreover, if
f=\mathds{1}_{B}
for some event
B\in\mathcal{F}
, we write the conditional expectation as
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{B\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{B}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]
.
More details on conditional expectations can be found in
Section

A.1
.
Moreover, we often make use of the concept of regular condition distributions.
For more details, see
Section

A.2
.
Lastly, for every bounded measurable region
\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{b}
, we write
\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}}:\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{N}
for the projection
\eta\mapsto\eta\cap\Lambda^{\prime}
.

Our main result in this section is the following statement.

Theorem 5.2
.

For all
t\in\mathds{N}_{0}
with
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathcal{U}_{t}=\emptyset}}}\right]>0
and all
A\in\mathfrak{R}
, it holds that

\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\in A\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\in A}\mathcal{U}_{t}=\emptyset}}\right.}}}\right]=\mu_{\Lambda}(A).

Since the algorithm terminates when
\mathcal{U}_{t}=\emptyset
, this implies that the output of
Algorithm

1
follows the distribution
\mu_{\Lambda}(A)
.
We deduce
Theorem

5.2
from the following invariant.

Lemma 5.3
.

For all
t\in\mathds{N}_{0}
,
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
with
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathcal{U}_{t}=S}}}\right]>0
and
A\in\mathfrak{R}
it holds that

\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\cap S^{c}\in A\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\cap S^{c}\in A}X_{t}\cap S,\mathcal{U}_{t}=S}}\right.}}}\right]=\mu_{S^{c}}^{X_{t}\cap S}(A).

In particular, the map

(\omega,A)\mapsto\mu_{S^{c}}^{X_{t}(\omega)\cap S}(A)\quad\text{$\omega\in\Omega$, $A\in\mathfrak{R}$}

is a regular conditional distribution of
X_{t}\cap S^{c}
given
\sigma(X_{t}\cap S)
under the probability measure
\mathds{P}_{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{U}_{t}=S}}\right\}}
.

Before we get into proving
Lemma

5.3
, we first show how
Theorem

5.2
follows from it.

Proof of
Theorem

5.2
.

Note that
X_{t}\cap\mathcal{V}=X_{t}
and
\sigma(X_{t}\cap\emptyset)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset,\Omega}}\right\}
for all
t\in\mathds{N}_{0}
.
Using
Lemma

5.3
for
S=\emptyset
, we obtain for all
A\in\mathfrak{R}

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\in A\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\in A}\mathcal{U}_{t}=\emptyset}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\cap\mathcal{V}\in A\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\cap\mathcal{V}\in A}X_{t}\cap\emptyset,\mathcal{U}_{t}=\emptyset}}\right.}}}\right]=\mu_{\Lambda}(A),

which proves the theorem.
∎

We proceed by stating and proving several lemmas that we will use to prove
Lemma

5.3
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Fix
t\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, and assume that for all
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
with
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathcal{U}_{t}=S}}}\right]>0
and all
A\in\mathfrak{R}
it holds that

\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\cap S^{c}\in A\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\cap S^{c}\in A}X_{t}\cap S,\mathcal{U}_{t}=S}}\right.}}}\right]=\mu_{S^{c}}^{X_{t}\cap S}(A).

Let
E=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{U}_{t}=S,\boldsymbol{u}_{t}=\boldsymbol{v}}}\right\}
for some
S\in 2^{\mathcal{V}}\setminus\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\}
and
\boldsymbol{v}\in S
such that
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{E}}}\right]>0
.
For any measurable region
\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq(\Lambda_{S})^{\mathrm{c}}
and any event
A\in\mathfrak{R}
it holds that

\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\prime}\in A\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\prime}\in A}X_{t}\cap S;E}}\right.}}}\right]=\mu_{S^{c}}^{X_{t}\cap S}\circ\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{-1}(A).

In particular,

(\omega,A)\mapsto\mu_{S^{c}}^{X_{t}(\omega)\cap S}\circ\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{-1}(A)\quad\text{$\omega\in\Omega$, $A\in\mathfrak{R}$}

is a regular conditional distribution of
X_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\prime}
given
\sigma(X_{t}\cap S)
under
\mathds{P}_{E}
.

Proof.

Fix some measurable region
{\Lambda^{\prime}}\subseteq(\Lambda_{S})^{\mathrm{c}}
and note that
\mu_{S^{c}}^{X_{t}\cap S}\circ\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{-1}
is a probability distribution on
(\mathcal{N},\mathfrak{R})
.
Thus, it suffices to show that
\mu_{S^{c}}^{X_{t}\cap S}\circ\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{-1}(A)
is also a version of the conditional expectation
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\prime}\in A\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\prime}\in A}X_{t}\cap S;E}}\right.}}}\right]
for all events
A\in\mathfrak{R}
.
By the assumptions of the lemma, we have

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\prime}\in A\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\prime}\in A}X_{t}\cap S;\mathcal{U}_{t}=S}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\cap S^{c}\in\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{-1}(A)\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\cap S^{c}\in\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{-1}(A)}X_{t}\cap S;\mathcal{U}_{t}=S}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mu_{S^{c}}^{X_{t}\cap S}\circ\pi_{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{-1}(A).

Next, we use
Lemma

A.3
part
2
to argue that this still holds if we change the probability measure from
\mathds{P}_{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{U}_{t}=S}}\right\}}
to
\mathds{P}_{E}
.
Note that, given
\mathcal{U}_{t}=S
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{t}
is chosen uniformly from
S
independent of
X_{t}
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\boldsymbol{u}_{t}=\boldsymbol{v}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\boldsymbol{u}_{t}=\boldsymbol{v}}X_{t}\cap S;\mathcal{U}_{t}=S}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\boldsymbol{u}_{t}=\boldsymbol{v}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\boldsymbol{u}_{t}=\boldsymbol{v}}\mathcal{U}_{t}=S}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\boldsymbol{u}_{t}=\boldsymbol{v}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\boldsymbol{u}_{t}=\boldsymbol{v}}X_{t}\cap S,X_{t}\cap\Lambda^{\prime};\mathcal{U}_{t}=S}}\right.}}}\right],

and applying
Lemma

A.3
part
2
proves the claim.
∎

We use the next lemma to prove
Lemma

5.3
in the case that
F_{t}=1
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Under the assumptions of
Lemma

5.4
, let
E_{1}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{U}_{t}=S,\boldsymbol{u}_{t}=\boldsymbol{v},F_{t}=1}}\right\}
for some
S\in 2^{\mathcal{V}}\setminus\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\}
and
\boldsymbol{v}\in S
such that
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{E_{1}}}}\right]>0
, and set
R=S\setminus\boldsymbol{v}
.
For all
A\in\mathfrak{R}

it holds that

\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t+1}\cap R^{c}\in A\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t+1}\cap R^{c}\in A}X_{t+1}\cap R;E_{1}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mu_{R^{c}}^{X_{t+1}\cap R}(A).

Proof.

An example that might help to keep track of the relevant regions throughout this proof is given in
Figure

2
.

Figure 2
:
Example for the regions considered throughout the proof of
Lemma

5.5
for
\ell=2
.

Let
E=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{U}_{t}=S,\boldsymbol{u}_{t}=\boldsymbol{v}}}\right\}
for
S
and
\boldsymbol{v}
as in the definition of
E_{1}
, and set
B=B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\ell}}\right)
and
H=\mathcal{V}\setminus(S\cup B)
.
Note that it suffices to consider
A\in\mathfrak{R}_{R^{c}}
.
Further, observe that
R^{c}
is the disjoint union of
H
and
B
.
Hence, by
Lemma

C.1
, it suffices to prove our claim for events of the form
\{X_{t+1}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B}\}
for pairs
A_{H}\in\mathfrak{R}_{H}
,
A_{B}\in\mathfrak{R}_{B}
.
Formally, we need to show

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t+1}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t+1}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B}}X_{t+1}\cap S;E_{1}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mu_{R^{c}}^{X_{t}\cap R}(\pi_{H}^{-1}(A_{H})\cap\pi_{B}^{-1}(A_{B})).

(6)

To establish (
6
), we derive a suitable version of the conditional expectation

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1}X_{t}\cap S;E}}\right.}}}\right].

(7)

Our first step is to obtain an expression for
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1}X_{t}\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c};E}}\right.}}}\right]
.
This is give by the following claim.

Claim 5.6
.

For

g:\omega\mapsto\mu_{B}^{X_{t}(\omega)\cap B^{c}}(A_{B})\,\text{ and }\\
h:\omega\mapsto C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},X_{t}(\omega)\cap S}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{X_{t}(\omega)\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{X_{t}(\omega)\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}

it holds that

\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1}X_{t}\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c};E}}\right.}}}\right](\omega)=g(\omega)\cdot h(\omega)

for
\mathds{P}_{E}
-almost all
\omega\in\Omega
.

Our next step is to derive an expression for the conditional expectation in (
7
) for every
A_{H}\in\mathfrak{R}_{H}
.
To this end, note that by
5.6
we have

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1}X_{t}\cap S;E}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H}}\cdot\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1}X_{t}\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c};E}}\right.}}}\right]\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H}}\cdot\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1}X_{t}\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c};E}}\right.}}}\right]}X_{t}\cap S;E}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H}}\cdot g\cdot h\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H}}\cdot g\cdot h}X_{t}\cap S;E}}\right.}}}\right],

(8)

where the first equality comes from the fact that
\sigma(X_{t}\cap S)\subseteq\sigma(X_{t}\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c})
.
Our goal is now to compute (
8
) by using
Theorem

A.4
, which tells us that the conditional expectation can be calculated by integrating
g\cdot h
over
A_{H}
against a regular conditional distribution for
X_{t}\cap H
given
\sigma(X_{t}\cap S)
under the measure
\mathds{P}_{E}
.
By
Lemma

5.4
, the map

(\omega,A)\mapsto\mu_{S^{c}}^{X_{t}(\omega)\cap S}\circ\pi_{H}^{-1}(A)\quad\text{ for $\omega\in\Omega$, $A\in\mathfrak{R}$}

is such a regular conditional distribution.
We obtain the following claim.

Claim 5.7
.

It holds that

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1}X_{t}\cap S;E}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad=C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},X_{t}\cap S}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{R^{c}}^{X_{t}\cap R}}{Z_{S^{c}}^{X_{t}\cap S}}\cdot\mu_{R^{c}}^{X_{t}\cap R}(\pi_{H}^{-1}(A_{H})\cap\pi_{B}^{-1}(A_{B}))

Given
5.7
, we proceed by using
Lemma

A.1
to switch from
\mathds{P}_{E}
to
\mathds{P}_{E_{1}}
.
To this end, note that

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{F_{t}=1}X_{t}\cap S;E}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\cap H\in\mathcal{N}_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in\mathcal{N}_{B},F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\cap H\in\mathcal{N}_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in\mathcal{N}_{B},F_{t}=1}X_{t}\cap S;E}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},X_{t}\cap S}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{R^{c}}^{X_{t}\cap R}}{Z_{S^{c}}^{X_{t}\cap S}}.

Thus, by
Lemma

A.1
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B}}X_{t}\cap S;E_{1}}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad=\frac{\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1}X_{t}\cap S;E}}\right.}}}\right]}{\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{F_{t}=1}X_{t}\cap S;E}}\right.}}}\right]}

\displaystyle\quad\quad=\mu_{R^{c}}^{X_{t}\cap R}(\pi_{H}^{-1}(A_{H})\cap\pi_{B}^{-1}(A_{B})).

As the right-hand side is
\sigma(X_{t}\cap R)
-measurable and
\sigma(X_{t}\cap R)\subseteq\sigma(X_{t}\cap S)
since
R\subseteq S
, we have

\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap\Lambda_{B}\in A_{B}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap\Lambda_{B}\in A_{B}}X_{t}\cap R;E_{1}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mu_{R^{c}}^{X_{t}\cap R}(\pi_{H}^{-1}(A_{H})\cap\pi_{B}^{-1}(A_{B}))

Moreover, given
E_{1}
, it holds that
X_{t+1}\cap H=X_{t}\cap H
and
X_{t+1}\cap R=X_{t}\cap R
, which proves (
6
).
Finally, applying
Lemma

C.1
as discussed earlier proves the lemma.
∎

Before we proceed with our main argument, we prove
5.6
and
5.7
.

Proof of
5.6
.

Note that given the event
E_{1}
, the point set
X_{t+1}\cap B
is sampled from
\mu_{B}^{X_{t}\cap B^{c}}
.
Therefore we have for all
A_{B}\in\mathfrak{R}_{B}
that

\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B}}X_{t}\cap B^{c};E_{1}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mu_{B}^{X_{t}\cap B^{c}}(A_{B})=g.

Since the above is true independently of the partial configuration
X_{t}\cap\boldsymbol{v}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B}}X_{t}\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c};E_{1}}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B}}X_{t}\cap B^{c},X_{t}\cap\boldsymbol{v};E_{1}}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B}}X_{t}\cap B^{c};E_{1}}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=g

Moreover, given
E
and
X_{t}\cap B^{c}
,
F_{t}
is drawn as a Bernoulli random variable with success probability

C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},X_{t}}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{X_{t}\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{X_{t}\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}},

where
C
is a Bayes filter correction as in
Definition

4.1
.
Thus, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{F_{t}=1}X_{t}\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c};E}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},X_{t}}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{X_{t}\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{X_{t}\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}

\displaystyle=C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},X_{t}\cap S}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{X_{t}\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{X_{t}\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}},

where the second equality holds since
C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\cdot}}\right)=C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\cdot\cap S}}\right)
by definition.
We note that the right-hand side of the last equality is precisely
h
.
By
Lemma

A.1
, we then have

\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1}X_{t}\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c};E}}\right.}}}\right](\omega)=g(\omega)\cdot h(\omega)

for
\mathds{P}_{E}
-almost all
\omega\in\Omega
.
∎

Proof of
5.7
.

Recall that by
5.6
and the fact that
\sigma(X_{t}\cap S)\subseteq\sigma(X_{t}\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c})
we have

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H},X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1}X_{t}\cap S;E}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H}}\cdot\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1}X_{t}\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c};E}}\right.}}}\right]\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H}}\cdot\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{t+1}\cap B\in A_{B},F_{t}=1}X_{t}\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c};E}}\right.}}}\right]}X_{t}\cap S;E}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H}}\cdot g\cdot h\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H}}\cdot g\cdot h}X_{t}\cap S;E}}\right.}}}\right].

Next, we define functions
\hat{g},\hat{h}:\mathcal{N}_{H}\times\mathcal{N}_{S}\to\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
via

\displaystyle\hat{g}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})

\displaystyle=\mu_{B}^{(\eta_{1}\cup\eta_{2})\cap B^{c}}(A_{B})

\displaystyle\hat{h}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})

\displaystyle=C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta_{2}}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{(\eta_{1}\cup\eta_{2})\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{(\eta_{1}\cup\eta_{2})\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}},

and observe that for all
\omega\in\Omega
it holds that
\hat{g}(X_{t}(\omega)\cap H,X_{t}(\omega)\cap S)=g(\omega)
and
\hat{h}(X_{t}(\omega)\cap H,X_{t}(\omega)\cap S)=h(\omega)
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H}}\cdot g\cdot h\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H}}\cdot g\cdot h}X_{t}\cap S;E}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H}}\cdot\hat{g}(X_{t}\cap H,X_{t}\cap S)\cdot\hat{h}(X_{t}\cap H,X_{t}\cap S)\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{X_{t}\cap H\in A_{H}}\cdot\hat{g}(X_{t}\cap H,X_{t}\cap S)\cdot\hat{h}(X_{t}\cap H,X_{t}\cap S)}X_{t}\cap S;E}}\right.}}}\right].

Further, by
Lemma

5.4
, the map

(\omega,A)\mapsto\mu_{S^{c}}^{X_{t}(\omega)\cap S}\circ\pi_{H}^{-1}(A)\quad\text{ for $\omega\in\Omega$, $A\in\mathfrak{R}$}

is a regular conditional distribution for
X_{t}\cap H
given
X_{t}\cap S
under the measure
\mathds{P}_{E}
.
Using
Theorem

A.4
then yields
