\hat{Z}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B,\gamma\cup(\eta\cap\Lambda_{S\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}),\delta_{2}}}\right)
, where
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
are as in
Lemma

7.4
.

In fact, we will not use
C_{\varepsilon}
directly for our Bayes filter, but rather a slightly scaled version
\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon}C_{\varepsilon}
, which is again a Bayes filter correction.
The slack due to the additional scaling allows us to efficiently sample the Bayes filter by using a Bernoulli factory, as we argue in the next lemma.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
be non-empty,
\boldsymbol{v}\in S
and
\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
be feasible, and set
B=B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\ell}}\right)
.
For all
\varepsilon>0
we can sample a Bernoulli random variable with success probability

\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon}C_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}

with expected running time only depending on
\varepsilon
,
\ell
,
r
,
\lambda
and
d
.

Proof.

Our goal is to use a Bernoulli factory of the form
\frac{p}{q}
to perform this task.
To bring the desired success probability into such a form, note that

\displaystyle Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})}}\right)^{-1}.

Moreover, note that
0\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon}C_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\leq 1
.
Thus, by setting
p=\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon}C_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\cdot\mu_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})
and
q=\mu_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})
we have
p\in[0,1],q\in[0,1]
and

\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon}C_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}=\frac{p}{q}.

We are now going to use
Lemma

3.1
to prove that we can obtain a sample from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p}{q}}}\right)
within the desired expected running time.
To this end, we need to provide a positive lower bound on
q-p
and we need an efficient way for generating independent samples from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)
and
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
.

For the lower bound, note that by
Lemma

C.2

Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}=Z_{B}^{\eta\cap B^{c}}
and
Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}=Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c}}
.
Moreover, since
C_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)
is a Bayes filter correction by
Lemma

7.4
and
\eta
is feasible, we have

C_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}\leq 1.

Consequently,
\frac{p}{q}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon}
and

\displaystyle q-p\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon}}}\right)q=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon}}}\right)\cdot(Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B})^{-1}\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon}}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda}.

Using the upper bound
\leq(2\ell+1)^{d}r^{d}
yields
q-p\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon}}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda(2\ell+1)^{d}r^{d}}
.

We proceed by arguing that we can obtain an oracle for
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
and
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)
as required by
Lemma

3.1
.
Firstly, note that by
7.6
we can compute
C_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)
with running time only depending on
\varepsilon
,
\ell
,
r
,
\lambda
and
d
.
After computing
C_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)
, each independent sample from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon}C_{\varepsilon}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)}}\right)
can be obtained in constant time.
Thus, it remains to argue that we can efficiently sample independent Bernoulli random variables with success probabilities
\mu_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})
and
\mu_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})
.
By
Lemma

7.5
, we can obtain independent samples from
\mu_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}
, each in expected time at most
(\lambda+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta\cap\partial B}}\right|)\cdot\lambda\mathrm{e}^{\lambda}
.
Note that
\leq(2\ell+1)^{d}r^{d}
and that, for feasible
\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta\cap\partial B}}\right|\leq(2\sqrt{d}/r)^{d}\leq(2\sqrt{d}/r)^{d}\cdot(2\ell+3)^{d}r^{d}
.
Therefore, the expected running time for obtaining independent Bernoulli samples with success probability
\mu_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})
is bounded by some function of
\varepsilon
,
\ell
,
r
,
\lambda
and
d
.
Treating
\mu_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})
analogously and applying
Lemma

3.1
now proves our claim.
∎

We conclude the following bound on the running time of each iteration.

Corollary 7.8
.

Suppose we run
Algorithm

1
on a hard-sphere model with
C(\cdot)=\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon}C_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
as Bayes filter correction in line
1
for some
\varepsilon>0
, and let
R_{t}
denote the running time of iteration
t\in\mathds{N}
.
Then, for all
t\in\mathds{N}
,
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{R_{t}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{R_{t}}X_{t-1},\mathcal{U}_{t-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1}}}\right.}}}\right]
is almost surely bounded by some function of
\varepsilon
,
\ell
,
r
,
\lambda
and
d
.

Proof.

Set
B=B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1},r}}\right)
, and note that the bulk of the running time in each iteration of
Algorithm

1
is due to sampling the Bayes filter in
algorithm

1
and updating the point configuration on
\Lambda_{B}
in
algorithm

1
.

For
algorithm

1
, note that
X_{t-1}
is almost surely feasible by
Lemma

4.3
.
Thus,
Lemma

7.7
yields that the expected time for sampling the Bayes filter, conditioned on
X_{t-1},\mathcal{U}_{t-1}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1}
, almost surely bounded by some function of
\varepsilon
,
\ell
,
r
,
\lambda
and
d
.

For
algorithm

1
, we can use
Lemma

7.5
to bound the expected time for sampling from
\mu_{B}^{X_{t-1}\cap B^{c}}
is bounded by
(\lambda+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t-1}\cap\partial B}}\right|)\cdot\lambda\mathrm{e}^{\lambda}
.
Note that
\leq(2\ell+1)^{d}r^{d}
and, if
X_{t-1}
is feasible,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t-1}\cap\partial B}}\right|\leq(2\sqrt{d}/r)^{d}\leq(2\sqrt{d}/r)^{d}\cdot(2\ell+3)^{d}r^{d}
.
Since
X_{t-1}
is indeed almost surely feasible, the expected running time for
algorithm

1
, conditioned on
X_{t-1},\mathcal{U}_{t-1}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1}
, is almost surely bounded by some function of
\varepsilon
,
\ell
,
r
,
\lambda
and
d
as well, which concludes the proof.
∎

We proceed by bounding the expected number of iterations of
Algorithm

1
, running on a hard-sphere model.
To this end, we start with the following lower bound on the success probability of the Bayes filter with correction
\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon}C_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
for a particular choice of
\varepsilon
.

Lemma 7.9
.

Consider a hard-sphere model that exhibits
(a,b)
-strong spatial mixing up to
\lambda
.
Then there are constants
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
, only depending on
a
,
b
,
r
,
\lambda
and
d
, such that for all non-empty
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
,
\boldsymbol{v}\in S
and feasible
\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
it holds that

\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}C_{\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}\geq 1-a^{\prime}\mathrm{e}^{-b^{\prime}\ell}.

Proof.

Set
\delta_{1}=\delta_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}}\right)
and
\delta_{2}=\delta_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}}\right)
as defined in
Lemma

7.4
.
Note that by
Lemma

C.2
we have

Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}=Z_{B}^{\eta\cap B^{c}}=Z_{B}^{(\eta\cap H)\cup(\eta\cap(S\setminus\boldsymbol{v}))},

where the last equality comes from the fact that
H
and
S\setminus\boldsymbol{v}
form a partitioning of
B^{c}
.
Similarly, we obtain

Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}=Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c}}=Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{(\eta\cap H)\cup(\eta\cap S)}.

Thus, applying
Lemma

6.1
with
\xi_{1}=\gamma
and
\xi_{2}=\eta\cap H
yields

\displaystyle\min_{\gamma\subseteq(\delta_{1}\mathds{Z})^{d}\cap\Lambda_{H\cap\partial B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\gamma\cup(\eta\cap S)}}{Z_{B}^{\gamma\cup(\eta\cap(S\setminus\boldsymbol{v}))}}}}\right\}\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt\geq\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({-a3^{d}r^{d}\mathrm{e}^{2br}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda r^{d}+\mathrm{e}^{\lambda 3^{d}r^{d}}}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-br\ell}}}}\right).

Moreover, following the same arguments as in
Lemma

7.4
, we have

\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}/2}\cdot\min_{\gamma\subseteq(\delta_{1}\mathds{Z})^{d}\cap\Lambda_{H\cap\partial B}}\frac{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\gamma\cup(\eta\cap S)}}{Z_{B}^{\gamma\cup(\eta\cap(S\setminus\boldsymbol{v}))}}\leq\min_{\gamma\subseteq(\delta_{1}\mathds{Z})^{d}\cap\Lambda_{H\cap\partial B}}\frac{\hat{Z}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v},\gamma\cup(\eta\cap S),\delta_{2}}}\right)}{\hat{Z}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B,\gamma\cup(\eta\cap(S\setminus\boldsymbol{v})),\delta_{2}}}\right)}.

Recalling the definition of
C_{\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)
in
Lemma

7.4
and noting that for
\ell\geq 2
it holds that
b\cdot(\ell-1)r\geq\frac{br}{2}\ell
, this implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}C_{\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({-a3^{d}r^{d}\mathrm{e}^{2br}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda r^{d}+\mathrm{e}^{\lambda 3^{d}r^{d}}}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-br\ell}}}}\right)\cdot\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({2\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq 1-a^{\prime}\mathrm{e}^{-b^{\prime}\ell}

for
b^{\prime}=\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,br}}\right\}
and
a^{\prime}=a3^{d}r^{d}\mathrm{e}^{2br}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda r^{d}+\mathrm{e}^{\lambda 3^{d}r^{d}}}}\right)+2
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma

7.9
allows us to control the success probability of the Bayes filter in terms of
\ell
.
This leads to the following statement.

Lemma 7.10
.

Consider a hard-sphere model that exhibits
(a,b)
-strong spatial mixing up to
\lambda
.
Suppose we run
Algorithm

1
with
C(\cdot)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}C_{\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}(\cdot)
as Bayes filter correction in line
1
, and let
T=\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}\mid\mathcal{U}_{t}=\emptyset}}\right\}
denote the number of iterations until the algorithm terminates.
Then, for
\ell
sufficiently large depending on
a
,
b
,
r
,
\lambda
and
d
, it holds that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T}}}\right]\leq 2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{V}}}\right|
.

Proof.

We aim for applying
Theorem

B.2
to prove our claim.
To this end, consider the process
(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|)_{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}}
and the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}}
defined by
\mathcal{F}_{t}=\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((X_{j},\mathcal{U}_{j},\boldsymbol{u}_{j})_{j\leq t}}}\right)
.
Further, observe that our desired hitting time can equivalently be written as
T=\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|\leq 0}}\right.}}\right\}
.
Since we are interested in the expectation of
T
, we only need to check assumptions
1
and
2
of
Theorem

B.2
.

For
1
of
Theorem

B.2
, observe that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|\geq 0
for all
t\in\mathds{N}_{0}
.
For
2
, we prove that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t+1}}}\right|)\mathds{1}_{T>t}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t+1}}}\right|)\mathds{1}_{T>t}}\mathcal{F}_{t}}}\right.}}}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathds{1}_{T>t}
if
\ell
is sufficiently large.
Since

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t+1}}}\right|)\mathds{1}_{T>t}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t+1}}}\right|)\mathds{1}_{T>t}}\mathcal{F}_{t}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|-\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t+1}}}\right|\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t+1}}}\right|}\mathcal{F}_{t}}}\right.}}}\right]}}\right)\mathds{1}_{T>t}

it suffices to show that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t+1}}}\right|\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t+1}}}\right|}\mathcal{F}_{t}}}\right.}}}\right]\mathds{1}_{T>t}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|-\frac{1}{2}}}\right)\mathds{1}_{T>t}
.

To simplify notation, we will omit the indicator of
T>t
while still restricting ourselves to the setting where
\mathcal{U}_{t}\neq\emptyset
.
Next, observe that, if
F_{t}=1
, then
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t+1}}}\right|=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|-1
.
On the other hand, if
F_{t}=0
, then

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t+1}}}\right|=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\partial B}}\right|\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|B\cup\partial B}}\right|\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\ell+3}}\right)^{d},

where
B=B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t},\boldsymbol{u}_{t},\ell}}\right)
.
Thus, it suffices if

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\ell+3}}\right)^{d}\cdot(1-\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{F_{t}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{F_{t}}\mathcal{F}_{t}}}\right.}}}\right])-\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{F_{t}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{F_{t}}\mathcal{F}_{t}}}\right.}}}\right]\leq-\frac{1}{2}.

Since, by
Lemma

7.9
,

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{F_{t}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{F_{t}}\mathcal{F}_{t}}}\right.}}}\right]\geq 1-a^{\prime}\mathrm{e}^{-b^{\prime}\ell},

for
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
only depending on
a
,
b
,
\lambda
,
r
and
d
, this is satisfied for
\ell
sufficiently large, depending on
a
,
b
,
\lambda
,
r
and
d
.
Applying
Theorem

B.2
then yields
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T}}}\right]\leq 2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{V}}}\right|
, which concludes the proof.
∎

We conclude the following theorem.

Theorem 7.11
.

Consider
Algorithm

1
on a hard-sphere model with
C(\cdot)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}C_{\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}(\cdot)
as Bayes filter correction in line
1
.
We can run the algorithm in almost-surely finite running time and, on termination, it outputs a sample from the hard-sphere Gibbs measure on
\Lambda
.
Moreover, if the hard-sphere model satisfies
(a,b)
-strong spatial mixing and if
\ell
is chosen as a sufficiently large constant, depending on
a
,
b
,
r
,
\lambda
and
d
, then we can run the algorithm in expected time
\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)
.

Proof.

For the first part of the statement, note that the correct output distribution follows directly from
Theorem

5.2
and the fact that
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}C_{\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}(\cdot)
is a Bayes filter correction by
Lemma

7.4
.
Let
T
denote that number of iterations of
Algorithm

1
, and let
R_{t}
denote the running time in iterations
t\in\mathds{N}
.
By
Corollary

5.1
we know that
T
is almost surely finite.
Moreover, it holds that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{R_{t}}}}\right]=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{R_{t}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{R_{t}}X_{t-1},\mathcal{U}_{t-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1}}}\right.}}}\right]}}}\right]
.
Since by
Corollary

7.8

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{R_{t}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{R_{t}}X_{t-1},\mathcal{U}_{t-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1}}}\right.}}}\right]\leq t(\ell,r,\lambda,d)
for some function
t:\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}^{3}\times\mathds{N}_{0}\to\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
, it also holds that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{R_{t}}}}\right]\leq t(\ell,r,\lambda,d)
.
Consequently,
R_{t}
must be finite almost surely, and
Algorithm

1
has almost surely finite running time.

For the second part of the statement, suppose the hard-sphere model satisfies
(a,b)
-strong spatial mixing up to
\lambda
.
Observe that the expected running time of
Algorithm

1
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\sum_{t=1}^{T}R_{t}}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\sum_{t\geq 1}\mathds{1}_{t\geq T}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{R_{t}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{R_{t}}X_{t-1},\mathcal{U}_{t-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1}}}\right.}}}\right]}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq t(\ell,r,\lambda,d)\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\sum_{t\geq 1}\mathds{1}_{t\geq T}}}}\right]

\displaystyle=t(\ell,r,\lambda,d)\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T}}}\right],

where the first equality uses the fact that
\mathds{1}_{t\geq T}=\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{U}_{t-1}\neq\emptyset}
is
\sigma(\mathcal{U}_{t-1})
-measurable.
By
Lemma

7.10
, we can choose
\ell
sufficiently large, depending on
a
,
b
,
r
,
\lambda
and
d
only, such that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T}}}\right]\leq 2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{V}}}\right|\in\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)
, proving linear expected running time of the algorithm for any such choice of
\ell
.
∎

8
General repulsive potentials

We now extend our perfect sampling algorithm to the setting of more general bounded-range repulsive potentials
\phi
.
In contrast to the hard-sphere model, it is not clear how to perform the minimization task in involved in constructing the Bayes filter in this setting.
We will instead assume knowledge of the rate of strong spatial mixing for constructing the Bayes filter.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
be non-empty and
\boldsymbol{v}\in S
.
Let
\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
be feasible and set
B=B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\ell}}\right)
.
Suppose
a,b>0
are such that
\phi
satisfies
(a,b)
-strong spatial mixing up to
\lambda
, and set

\delta=\delta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(a,b}}\right)\coloneqq\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({-a3^{d}r^{d}\mathrm{e}^{2br}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda r^{d}+\mathrm{e}^{\lambda 3^{d}r^{d}}}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-br\ell}}}}\right)

and

C_{a,b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\coloneqq\delta\cdot\frac{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap S}}{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap(S\setminus\boldsymbol{v})}}.

Then
C_{a,b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)
is a Bayes filter correction as in
Definition

4.1
.
Moreover, it holds that

\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({-2a3^{d}r^{d}\mathrm{e}^{2br}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda r^{d}+\mathrm{e}^{\lambda 3^{d}r^{d}}}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-br\ell}}}}\right)\leq C_{a,b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}\leq 1.

Proof.

We start by checking that
C_{a,b}(\cdot)
is a Bayes filter correction.
For the measurability, note that for all non-empty
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
,
\boldsymbol{v}\in S
and
\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
it holds that
C_{a,b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)
does only depend on
\eta\cap S
.
Moreover, observe that

\displaystyle C_{a,b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({-a3^{d}r^{d}\mathrm{e}^{2br}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda r^{d}+\mathrm{e}^{\lambda 3^{d}r^{d}}}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-br\ell}}}}\right)\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda}

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({-a3^{d}r^{d}\mathrm{e}^{2br}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda r^{d}+\mathrm{e}^{\lambda 3^{d}r^{d}}}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-br\ell}}}}\right)\cdot\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({-\lambda(2\ell+3)^{d}r^{d}}}}\right)

uniformly in
\eta
.
For the upper bound, we apply
Lemma

6.1
with
\xi_{1}=\emptyset
and
\xi_{2}=\xi
to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap S}}{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap(S\setminus\boldsymbol{v})}}\cdot\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\mathcal{N}_{H}\\
\xi\cup(\eta\cap S)\text{ is feasible}\end{subarray}}\frac{Z_{B}^{\xi\cup(\eta\cap(S\setminus\boldsymbol{v}))}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\xi\cup(\eta\cap S)}}\leq\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({a3^{d}r^{d}\mathrm{e}^{2br}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda r^{d}+\mathrm{e}^{\lambda 3^{d}r^{d}}}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-br\ell}}}}\right).

Therefore, multiplying both sides with
\delta
yields

C_{a,b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\leq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\mathcal{N}_{H}\\
\xi\cup(\eta\cap S)\text{ is feasible}\end{subarray}}\frac{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\xi\cup(\eta\cap S)}}{Z_{B}^{\xi\cup(\eta\cap(S\setminus\boldsymbol{v}))}}

as desired.

For the second part of the statement, note that by
Lemma

C.2
we have

Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}=Z_{B}^{\eta\cap B^{c}}=Z_{B}^{(\eta\cap H)\cup(\eta\cap(S\setminus\boldsymbol{v}))},

where the last equality comes from the fact that
H
and
S\setminus\boldsymbol{v}
form a partitioning of
B^{c}
.
Similarly, we obtain

Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}=Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(B\setminus\boldsymbol{v})^{c}}=Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{(\eta\cap H)\cup(\eta\cap S)}.

Since
C_{a,b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)
is a Bayes filter correction, it follows that

C_{a,b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}\leq 1.

Moreover, applying
Lemma

6.1
with
\xi_{1}=\emptyset
and
\xi_{2}=\eta\cap\Lambda_{H}
yields

\displaystyle\frac{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap S}}{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap(S\setminus\boldsymbol{v})}}\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}\geq\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({-a3^{d}r^{d}\mathrm{e}^{2br}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda r^{d}+\mathrm{e}^{\lambda 3^{d}r^{d}}}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-br\ell}}}}\right)

and multiplying both sides with
\delta
proves the claim.
∎

While the first part of
Lemma

8.1
is sufficient to guarantee correctness of
Algorithm

1
, the second part of the statement will be useful for bounding the running by allowing us to control the probability of the event
F_{t}=0
for each iteration
t\in\mathds{N}_{0}
.
Similarly as in the setting of the hard-sphere model, we will not work directly with
C_{a,b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)
, but rather use a slightly scaled version, which is a Bayes filter correction in its own right.

Corollary 8.2
.

In the setting of
Lemma

8.1
, it holds that
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}C_{a,b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},r}}\right)
is a Bayes filter correction, and there are constants
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
, only depending on
a
,
b
,
r
,
\lambda
and
d
, such that

1-a^{\prime}\mathrm{e}^{-b^{\prime}\ell}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}C_{a,b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}.

Proof.

Since
0<\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}\leq 1
, it is obvious that
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}C_{a,b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},r}}\right)
satisfies
Definition

4.1
.
Now, note that for
\ell\geq 2
it holds that
b(\ell-1)r\geq\frac{br}{2}\ell
.
The statement directly follows from
Lemma

8.1
by setting
b^{\prime}=\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,br}}\right\}
and
a^{\prime}=2a3^{d}r^{d}\mathrm{e}^{2br}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda r^{d}+\mathrm{e}^{\lambda 3^{d}r^{d}}}}\right)+1
, and observing that
\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({-a^{\prime}\mathrm{e}^{-b^{\prime}\ell}}}}\right)\geq 1-a^{\prime}\mathrm{e}^{-b^{\prime}\ell}
.
∎

Next, we focus on how to sample the Bayes filter, using
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}C_{a,b}(\cdot)
as in
Corollary

8.2
as Bayes filter correction.
In contrast to our approach for the hard-sphere model, we do not know how to compute
C_{a,b}(\cdot)
directly.
Again, we solve this problem using a Bernoulli factory.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
be non-empty,
\boldsymbol{v}\in S
and
\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
be feasible, and set
B=B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\ell}}\right)
.
Suppose
a,b>0
are such that
\phi
satisfies
(a,b)
-strong spatial mixing up to
\lambda
and let
C_{a,b}(\cdot)
be as in
Lemma

8.1
.
We can sample a Bernoulli random variable with success probability

\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}C_{a,b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}

with expected running time in
\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v}}}\right|}}}\right)
, where the constants only depend on
\ell
,
r
,
\lambda
and
d
.

Proof.

Our goal is to use a Bernoulli factory of the form
\frac{p}{q}
to perform this task.
To bring the desired success probability into such a form, note that

\displaystyle Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})}}\right)^{-1}.

Moreover, using
Lemma

C.2
we have

\displaystyle Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap S}

\displaystyle=Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap((\partial B\cap S)\cup\boldsymbol{v})}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap((\partial B\cap S)\cup\boldsymbol{v})}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle Z_{B}^{\eta\cap(S\setminus\boldsymbol{v})}

\displaystyle=Z_{B}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cap S)}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{B}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cap S)}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})}}\right)^{-1}.

Finally, note that
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}\in[0,1]
and, for
\delta=\delta
as in
Lemma

8.1
,
\delta\in[0,1]
.
Thus, by setting

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}\delta\cdot\mu_{B}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cap S)}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})\cdot\mu_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mu_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap((\partial B\cap S)\cup\boldsymbol{v})}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})\cdot\mu_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})

we have
p\in[0,1],q\in[0,1]
and

\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}C_{a,b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}=\frac{p}{q}.

We are now going to use
Lemma

3.1
to prove that we can obtain a sample from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p}{q}}}\right)
within the desired expected running time.
To this end, we need to provide a positive lower bound on
q-p
and we need an efficient way for generating independent samples from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)
and
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
.

For the lower bound, note that by
Corollary

8.2
it holds that

\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}C_{a,b}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{v},\eta}}\right)\cdot\frac{Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}{Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}.

Consequently, we have

\displaystyle q-p

\displaystyle\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}}}\right)q

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}}}\right)\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap((\partial B\cap S)\cup\boldsymbol{v})}\cdot Z_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda}.

Using the upper bound
\leq(2\ell+1)^{d}r^{d}
yields
q-p\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda(2\ell+1)^{d}r^{d}}
.

We proceed by arguing that we can obtain an oracle for
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
and
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)
as required by
Lemma

3.1
.
In particular, we focus on
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
since
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)
can be treated analogously.
Firstly, note that we can sample a Bernoulli random variable with success probability
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}\delta
in constant time, since we can compute it explicitly.
It remains to argue that we can sample Bernoulli random variables with success probability
\mu_{B}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cap S)}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})
and
\mu_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})
in the desired running time.
Again, we focus on
\mu_{B}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cap S)}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})
and treat
\mu_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})
analogously.
By
Lemma

7.5
, we can obtain independent samples from
\mu_{B}^{\eta\cap\partial B}
, each in expected time at most
(\lambda+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta\cap\partial B}}\right|)\cdot\lambda\mathrm{e}^{\lambda}
.
Noting that
\leq(2\ell+1)^{d}r^{d}
and that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta\cap\partial B}}\right|\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}\right|
yields an expected running time of
\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}\right|}}}\right)
.
Applying the same argument to sample a Bernoulli random variable with success probability
\mu_{B\setminus\boldsymbol{v}}^{\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\emptyset}}\right\})
yields an oracle for
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
with expected running time in
\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{v})}}\right|}}}\right)
.
Finally, applying the same procedure for
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)
and using
Lemma

3.1
concludes the proof.
∎

We obtain the following bound for the running time of each iteration.

Corollary 8.4
.

Let
a,b>0
be such that
\phi
satisfies
(a,b)
-strong spatial mixing up to
\lambda
.
Suppose we run
Algorithm

1
on
\phi
with
C(\cdot)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}C_{a,b}(\cdot)
as Bayes filter correction in line
1
, and let
R_{t}
denote the running time for iteration
t\in\mathds{N}
.
Then, for all
t\in\mathds{N}
, it holds that

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{R_{t}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{R_{t}}X_{t-1},\mathcal{U}_{t-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1}}}\right.}}}\right]\leq\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t-1}\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1})}}\right|}}}\right),

where
B=B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1},\ell}}\right)
, and the constants in the asymptotic notation only depend on
\ell
,
r
,
\lambda
and
d
.

Proof.

Set
B=B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1},\ell}}\right)
, and note that the bulk of the running time in each iteration of
Algorithm

1
is due to sampling the Bayes filter in
algorithm

1
and updating the point configuration on
\Lambda_{B}
in
algorithm

1
.

For
algorithm

1
, note that
X_{t-1}
is almost surely feasible by
Lemma

4.3
.
Thus,
Lemma

8.3
yields that the expected time for sampling the Bayes filter, conditioned on
X_{t-1},\mathcal{U}_{t-1}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1}
, almost surely bounded by some function in
\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t-1}\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1})}}\right|}}}\right)
.
Further, for
algorithm

1
, we can use
Lemma

7.5
to bound the expected time for sampling from
\mu_{B}^{X_{t-1}\cap B^{c}}
is bounded by
(\lambda+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t-1}\cap\partial B}}\right|)\cdot\lambda\mathrm{e}^{\lambda}\in\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t-1}\cap(\partial B\cup\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1})}}\right|}}}\right)
as desired.
∎

Next, we derive a bound on the expected number of iterations of
Algorithm

1
given strong spatial mixing.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
a,b>0
be such that
\phi
satisfies
(a,b)
-strong spatial mixing up to
\lambda
.
Suppose we run
Algorithm

1
with
C(\cdot)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}C_{a,b}(\cdot)
as Bayes filter correction in line
1
, and let
T=\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}\mid\mathcal{U}_{t}=\emptyset}}\right\}
denote the number of iterations until the algorithm terminates.
Then, for
\ell
sufficiently large depending on
a
,
b
,
r
,
\lambda
and
d
, it holds that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T}}}\right]\leq 2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{V}}}\right|
, and for all
k\geq 4\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{V}}}\right|
it holds that
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T\geq k}}}\right]\leq\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({-\frac{k}{\alpha}}}}\right)
for some constant
\alpha\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
that only depends on
\ell
,
d
and
r
.

Proof.

We aim for applying
Theorem

B.2
to prove our claim.
For bounding
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T}}}\right]
, we proceed analogously as in the proof of
Lemma

7.10
.
In particular, we consider the process
(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|)_{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}}
with the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}}
defined by
\mathcal{F}_{t}=\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((X_{j},\mathcal{U}_{j},\boldsymbol{u}_{j})_{j\leq t}}}\right)
and rewrite our desired hitting time as
T=\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|\leq 0}}\right.}}\right\}
.
Since
1
of
Theorem

B.2
is trivially satisfied, we only need to check
2
.
Using the lower bound from
Corollary

8.2
and the same arguments as in the proof of
Lemma

7.10
, we can show that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t+1}}}\right|)\mathds{1}_{T>t}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t+1}}}\right|)\mathds{1}_{T>t}}\mathcal{F}_{t}}}\right.}}}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathds{1}_{T>t}
if
\ell
is sufficiently large, depending on
a
,
b
,
r
,
\lambda
and
d
.
Thus, applying the first part of
Theorem

B.2
proves our bound on
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T}}}\right]
.

To obtain the tail bound on
T
, we apply the second part of
Theorem

B.2
.
For
3
, note that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{0}}}\right|=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{V}}}\right|
and, for
4
, observe that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t}}}\right|-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{U}_{t-1}}}\right|}}\right|\leq\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,(2\ell+3)^{d}r^{d}}}\right\}.

Thus, setting
\alpha\geq 64\cdot\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,(2\ell+3)^{2d}r^{2d}}}\right\}
concludes the proof.
∎

Note that, in contrast to
Corollary

7.8
,
Corollary

8.4
does not give a deterministic bound on the running time of each iteration.
Due to potential dependencies between the running time of each iteration and termination of the algorithm, it is unclear if we can simply apply Wald’s equation to derive the total running time of the algorithm.
Instead, we will use a more subtle argument for this.
As a first ingredient, we need upper-bound the probability of ever observing a large number of points in any box
\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}
for
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{V}
up to a given iteration
k\in\mathds{N}_{0}
.

Lemma 8.6
.

There is a constant
\alpha\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 1}
, only depending on
\ell
,
d
and
r
, such that, for all
k\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, all
\gamma>1
and all
x\geq\mathrm{e}^{\gamma}\lambda r^{d}
, it holds that

\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bigcup_{t=0}^{k}\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}}}}\right]\leq(k+1)\alpha\mathrm{e}^{-(\gamma-1)x}.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\ell+1}}\right)^{d}
.
We show the statement via induction over
k
.
First, for
k=0
, note that
X_{0}=\emptyset
.
Thus, we have that the left-hand side is
0
for all
x>0
and the right-hand side is at least
1
for
x=0
, proving the base case.

Next, assume the statement holds for some fixed
k\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 0}
.
Let
\hat{B}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
be the set of boxes updated in iteration
k+1
(possibly the empty set).
Formally, that is
\hat{B}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{V}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{V}}X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}\neq X_{k}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right.}}\right\}
.
By the induction hypothesis and union bound, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bigcup_{t=0}^{k+1}\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bigcup_{t=0}^{k}\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}\cup\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\exists\boldsymbol{v}\in\hat{B}\text{ s.t. }\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bigcup_{t=0}^{k}\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}}}}\right]+\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\exists\boldsymbol{v}\in\hat{B}\text{ s.t. }\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(k+1)\alpha\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-(\gamma-1)x}+\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\exists\boldsymbol{v}\in\hat{B}\text{ s.t. }\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}}}}\right].

It now suffices to show that

\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\exists\boldsymbol{v}\in\hat{B}\text{ s.t. }\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}}}}\right]\leq\alpha\cdot\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{Y\geq x}}}\right].

First, note that
\hat{B}=\emptyset
if
F_{k}=0
.
Thus, we have

\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\exists\boldsymbol{v}\in\hat{B}\text{ s.t. }\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\exists\boldsymbol{v}\in\hat{B}\text{ s.t. }\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}}F_{k}=0}}\right.}}}\right]\leq\alpha\cdot\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{Y\geq x}}}\right]

for all
x\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Now, fix
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
and
\boldsymbol{w}\in S
such that
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathcal{U}_{k}=S,\boldsymbol{u}_{k}=\boldsymbol{w},F_{k}=1}}}\right]>0
.
Set
B=B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S,\boldsymbol{w},\ell}}\right)
and observe that, given
\mathcal{U}_{k}=S
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{k}=\boldsymbol{u}
and
F_{k}=1
, it holds that
\hat{B}=B
.
Using union bound, we have

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\exists\boldsymbol{v}\in\hat{B}\text{ s.t. }\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\exists\boldsymbol{v}\in\hat{B}\text{ s.t. }\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}}\mathcal{U}_{k}=S,\boldsymbol{u}_{k}=\boldsymbol{w},F_{k}=1}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad=\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{v}\in B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{v}\in B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}}\mathcal{U}_{k}=S,\boldsymbol{u}_{k}=\boldsymbol{w},F_{k}=1}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\leq\sum_{\boldsymbol{v}\in B}\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}\mathcal{U}_{k}=S,\boldsymbol{u}_{k}=\boldsymbol{w},F_{k}=1}}\right.}}}\right].

Given
\mathcal{U}_{k}=S
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{k}=\boldsymbol{w}
and
F_{k}=1
, it holds that
X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{B}
is sampled from
\mu_{B}^{\eta\cap B^{c}}
for some feasible
\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
.
Therefore, for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in B
,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|
is dominated by a Poisson random variable
Y
with parameter
\lambda\leq\lambda r^{d}
.
Further, observing that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|B}}\right|\leq\alpha
yields

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\exists\boldsymbol{v}\in\hat{B}\text{ s.t. }\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\exists\boldsymbol{v}\in\hat{B}\text{ s.t. }\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}}\mathcal{U}_{k}=S,\boldsymbol{u}_{k}=\boldsymbol{w},F_{k}=1}}\right.}}}\right]\leq\alpha\cdot\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{Y\geq x}}}\right].

Using the law of total expectation and
Corollary

B.4
, we obtain

\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\exists\boldsymbol{v}\in\hat{B}\text{ s.t. }\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{k+1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|\geq x}}\right\}}}}\right]\leq\alpha\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-(\gamma-1)x},

which proves the claim.
∎

Using
Lemma

8.6
, we derive our main result on perfect sampling for repulsive bounded-range potentials based on
Algorithm

1
.

Theorem 8.7
.

Let
a,b>0
be such that
\phi
satisfies
(a,b)
-strong spatial mixing up to
\lambda
.
Consider
Algorithm

1
with
C(\cdot)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}C_{a,b}(\cdot)
as Bayes filter correction in line
1
.
On termination, the algorithm outputs a sample from the Gibbs measure
\mu_{\lambda,\Lambda}
.
Moreover, if
\ell
is chosen as a sufficiently large constant, depending on
a
,
b
,
r
,
\lambda
and
d
, then we can run the algorithm in expected time
\widetilde{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)
.

Proof.

If
\phi
satisfies
(a,b)
-strong spatial mixing up to
\lambda
, we know by
Corollary

8.2
that
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-\ell}}C_{a,b}(\cdot)
is a Bayes filter correction.
Thus, the first part of the statement follows from
Theorem

5.2
.

Next, let
\ell
be chosen as a sufficiently large constant, depending on
a,b,r,\lambda
and
d
to satisfy
Lemma

8.5
.
We rewrite the running time of
Algorithm

1
as

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\sum_{t=1}^{T}R_{t}}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{T\geq t}R_{t}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{T\geq t}R_{t}}\mathcal{U}_{t-1},X_{t-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1}}}\right.}}}\right]}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{T\geq t}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{R_{t}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{R_{t}}\mathcal{U}_{t-1},X_{t-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1}}}\right.}}}\right]}}}\right]

Moreover, by
Corollary

8.4
, there are constants
a_{1},a_{2}
, only depending on
\ell,d,\lambda
and
r
, such that

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{R_{t}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{R_{t}}\mathcal{U}_{t-1},X_{t-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1}}}\right.}}}\right]\leq\alpha_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t-1}\cap(\partial B_{t}\cup\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1})}}\right|+\alpha_{2}

where
B_{t}=B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{U}_{t-1},\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1},\ell}}\right)
.
Thus, we have

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\sum_{t=1}^{T}R_{t}}}}\right]\leq\alpha_{1}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{T\geq t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t-1}\cap(\partial B_{t}\cup\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1})}}\right|}}}\right]+\alpha_{2}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{T\geq t}}}}\right].

Note that by
Lemma

8.5

\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{T\geq t}}}}\right]=\sum_{t^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{T>t^{\prime}}}}}\right]=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T}}}\right]\in\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right).

It remains to bound

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{T\geq t}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t-1}\cap(\partial B_{t}\cup\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1})}}\right|}}}\right].

To this end, write
W=\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{T\geq t}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t-1}\cap(\partial B_{t}\cup\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1})}}\right|
.
Since
W
is non-negative, we have

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{W}}}\right]=\int_{\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}}\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{W>w}}}\right]\,\text{d}w\leq\hat{w}+\int_{\hat{w}}^{\infty}\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{W>w}}}\right]\,\text{d}w

for every
\hat{w}\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Next, observe that for every
t\in\mathds{N}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\partial B_{t}\cup\boldsymbol{u}_{t-1}}}\right|\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\partial B_{t}\cup B_{t}}}\right|\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\ell+1}}\right)^{d}\eqqcolon\alpha_{3}.

Thus, for every
w\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
and every
k:\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathds{N}
the following holds: if
W>w
, then
T>k(w)
or there is a time point
t\leq k(w)
and a box
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{V}
such that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t-1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|>\frac{w}{k(w)\alpha_{3}}
.
Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{W>w}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{T>k(w)}}\right\}\cup\bigcup_{t=1}^{k(w)}\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t-1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|>\frac{w}{k(w)\alpha_{3}}}}\right\}}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T>k(w)}}}\right]+\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bigcup_{t=1}^{k(w)}\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t-1}\cap\Lambda_{\boldsymbol{v}}}}\right|>\frac{w}{k(w)\alpha_{3}}}}\right\}}}}\right].

Moreover, applying
Lemma

8.6
shows that there is a constant
\alpha_{4}
, only depending on
\ell
,
r
and
d
, such that for all
w\geq\mathrm{e}^{2}\alpha_{3}\lambda r^{d}k(w)
it holds that

\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{W>w}}}\right]\leq\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T\geq k(w)}}}\right]+(k(w)-1)\alpha_{4}\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({-\frac{w}{k(w)\alpha_{3}}}}}\right),

Now, suppose that
k
is measurable, we get for
\hat{w}\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{W}}}\right]\leq\hat{w}+\int_{\hat{w}}^{\infty}\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T\geq k(w)}}}\right]\,\text{d}w+\alpha_{4}\int_{\hat{w}}^{\infty}k(w)\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({-\frac{w}{k(w)\alpha_{3}}}}}\right)\,\text{d}w.

We now claim that for a suitable choice of
\hat{w}
and
k
the right-hand side is in
\widetilde{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)
.

To this end, let
k(w)=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil 4\mathrm{e}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{V}}}\right|}}\right\rceil,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil 2\alpha_{5}\ln(w)}}\right\rceil}}\right\}
, where
\alpha_{5}
is the constant from the tail bound in
Lemma

8.5
, which only depends on
\ell,r
and
d
.
Moreover, choose
\hat{w}\geq 1
sufficiently large such that for all
w\geq\hat{w}
it holds that
w\geq k(w)
,
w\geq\mathrm{e}^{2}\alpha_{3}\lambda r^{d}k(w)
and
\frac{w}{\ln(w)}\geq 3\alpha_{3}k(w)
.
Note that this can be achieved for some
\hat{w}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{V}}}\right|}}}\right)
.

For our choice of
k(w)
,
Lemma

8.5
yields

\int_{\hat{w}}^{\infty}\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T\geq k(w)}}}\right]\,\text{d}w\leq\int_{\hat{w}}^{\infty}w^{-2}\,\text{d}w,

which is bounded by
1
for
\hat{w}\geq 1
.
Moreover, for
w\geq k(w)
and
\frac{w}{\ln(w)}\geq 3\alpha_{3}k(w)
we have

\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({-\frac{w}{k(w)\alpha_{3}}}}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{k(w)w^{2}}

and thus

\int_{\hat{w}}^{\infty}k(w)\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({-\frac{w}{k(w)\alpha_{3}}}}}\right)\,\text{d}w\leq\int_{\hat{w}}^{\infty}w^{-2}\,\text{d}w\leq 1.

Consequently, we have

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{W}}}\right]\leq\hat{w}+\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({1}}}\right)\in\widetilde{\mathrm{O}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{V}}}\right|}}}\right)

and, since
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathcal{V}}}\right|\in\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({}}}\right)
, this concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 8.8
.

Since
Theorem

8.7
requires knowing constants
a,b
such that the Point process satisfies
(a,b)
-strong spatial mixing (in contrast to
Theorem

7.11
), we can use these constant to compute a sufficiently large value for
\ell
.
Elementary calculations suggest to choose
\ell>\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1+7r}{2},16a^{\prime}{b^{\prime}}^{2}}}\right\}
, where
b^{\prime}=\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,br}}\right\}
and
a^{\prime}=2a3^{d}r^{d}\mathrm{e}^{2br}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda r^{d}+\mathrm{e}^{\lambda 3^{d}r^{d}}}}\right)+1
.

9
Bernoulli Factories

In this section we prove
Lemma

3.1
, showing how to sample a random variable from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p}{q}}}\right)
given access to a
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
and
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)
sampler, when
q-p>\epsilon
. This happens in the following three steps.

The first step is to sample a random variable according to
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-(q-p)}{2}}}\right)
given access to
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
and
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)
. This is achieved by
Algorithm

3
below.

Draw
u\sim\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1/2}}\right)

if

u=1

then

1

Draw
y\sim\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)

return

1-y

else

2

Draw
x\sim\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)

return

x

Algorithm 3

\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-(q-p)}{2}}}\right)
from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
and
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)

It is easy to verify that this algorithm returns 1 with the correct probability.

For the second step, let
\varrho=\frac{1-(q-p)}{2}
. From
Algorithm

3
we now assume to have access to a
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varrho}}\right)
random variable. The next step is to use Huber’s algorithm
[
37
]
and obtain a
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\varrho}}\right)
. For the algorithm to work within the required run-time guarantees, we need
\varrho<\frac{1-\epsilon}{2}
, which holds since we assumed
q-p>\epsilon
. For convenience, we provide the pseudocode of Huber’s algorithm in
Algorithm

4
. The correctness of the algorithm can be found in
[
37
, Section 2.3]
.

\epsilon\leftarrow\min\{\epsilon,0.644\}
,

k\leftarrow\frac{23}{5\epsilon}
,

i\leftarrow 1
,

R\leftarrow 1
,

C\leftarrow 2

while

i\neq 0
and
R\neq 0

do

1

while

0<i<k

do

2

Draw
u\sim\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varrho}}\right)

Draw
g\sim Geo\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{C-1}{C}}}\right)

i\leftarrow i-1+(1-u)g

if

i\geq k

then

3

Draw
R\sim Ber\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}}\right)^{-i}}}\right)

if

R=0

then

4

return

0

C\leftarrow C(1+\frac{\epsilon}{2})
,

\epsilon\leftarrow\frac{\epsilon}{2}
,

k\leftarrow 2k

return

1

Algorithm 4

\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\varrho}}\right)
from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varrho}}\right)

Algorithm

4
now gives us access to a
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\varrho}}\right)
sampler and consequently to a
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-2\varrho}}\right)=\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q-p}}\right)
sampler, simply by flipping the returned bit. The final step is to sample from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p}{q}}}\right)
when given access to a sampler for
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q-p}}\right)
. This is done via
Algorithm

5
.

Set
b=-1

while

b=-1

do

1

Draw
u_{1}\sim\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1/2}}\right)

if

u_{1}=1

then

2

Draw
x\sim\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)

if

x=1

then

3

Set
b=1

else

4

Draw
y\sim\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q-p}}\right)

if

y=1

then

5

Set
b=0

return

b

Algorithm 5

\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{p}{q}}}\right)
from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
and
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q-p}}\right)

Regarding the correctness of
Algorithm

5
note that, within a single while-loop, the probability the algorithm returns 1 is
p/2
, while the probability that the algorithm enters the while-loop again is
1-q/2
. Conditioned on the fact that the algorithm will terminate, we observe that
Algorithm

5
returns 1 with probability
p/q
.

We are now ready to prove
Lemma

3.1
, whose statement we repeat here for convinience.

See
3.1

Proof.

We use
Algorithm

5
which calls
Algorithm

4
, which in turn calls
Algorithm

3
, as we explained above.
For simplicity, we may assume that, every time we sample
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
, we also sample from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)
and vice versa.
Thus, let
(X_{i})_{i\in\mathds{N}}\in(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0,1}}\right\}^{2})^{\mathds{N}}
be a sequence of samples from the product distribution
(\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)\otimes\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right))^{\otimes\mathds{N}}
and assume that
X_{i}=(X_{i}^{(p)},X_{i}^{(q)})
is the outcome of the
i
th time the algorithm samples from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
and
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)
.
Moreover, let
(T_{i})_{i\in\mathds{N}}
be the running time for obtaining the
X_{i}
using the assumed oracle, and let
N
denote the total number of samples from
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
and
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)
that
Algorithm

5
requires.

Our goal is to show that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\sum_{i=1}^{N}T_{i}}}}\right]\in\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({t\epsilon^{-2}}}}\right)
.
To this end, note that, for every
i\in\mathds{N}
, the event
N\geq i
does only depend on the sequence
(X_{i})_{i\in\mathds{N}}
via the subsequence
(X_{j})_{j<i}
.
Moreover, by our assumptions on the oracle, it holds that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T_{i}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{T_{i}}(X_{j})_{j<i}}}\right.}}}\right]\leq 2t
, where the factor of
2
comes from the fact that we sample both
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
and
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q}}\right)
.
Thus, by Wald’s equation, we have

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\sum_{i=1}^{N}T_{i}}}}\right]=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\sum_{i\geq 1}\mathds{1}_{N\geq i}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T_{i}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{T_{i}}(X_{j})_{j<i}}}\right.}}}\right]}}}\right]\leq 2t\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{N}}}\right].

It remains to show that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{N}}}\right]\in\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\epsilon^{-2}}}}\right)
.

Algorithm

5
will do
2/q\in\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\epsilon^{-1}}}}\right)
while-loops in expectation, as each while-loop terminates with probability
q/2
. Furthermore, each while-loop calls either
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p}}\right)
or
Algorithm

4
, both with probability
1/2
. As the total number of loops is determined by the outcome of the final loop, we can use Wald’s equation again to get that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{N}}}\right]\in\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\epsilon^{-1}+\epsilon^{-1}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{N^{\prime}}}}\right]}}}\right)
, where
N^{\prime}
is the number of
X_{i}
samples that
Algorithm

4
requires. Observe (from the pseudocode of
Algorithm

3
) that each
\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varrho}}\right)
-call
Algorithm

4
requires only a single
X_{i}
sample. From
[
37
, Theorem 1.1]
we get that
Algorithm

4
requires at most
\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\epsilon^{-1}}}}\right)

\mathrm{Ber}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varrho}}\right)
samples in expectation, which implies that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{N^{\prime}}}}\right]\in\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\epsilon^{-1}}}}\right)
. This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Appendix A
Measure theory and conditional expectations

A.1
Conditional expectation

We start with a brief recap of the notation used in the appendix.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathds{P})
be a probability space.
For an event
A\in\mathcal{A}
with
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{A}}}\right]>0
, we write
\mathds{P}_{A}
for the probability measure
\mathds{P}_{A}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\,\cdot}}}\right]=\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\,\cdot\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\,\cdot}A}}\right.}}}\right]

on
(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
.
Note that for all events
A,B\in\mathcal{A}
with
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{A\cap B}}}\right]>0
it holds that
\mathds{P}_{A\cap B}=(\mathds{P}_{A})_{B}
.
Let
f,g:\Omega\to\mathds{R}
be measurable maps.
We denote by
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f}}}\right]
the expectation of
f
under the measure
\mathds{P}
.
For a sub-
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, we write
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{f}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]
as a placeholder for any version of a conditional expectation of
f
given
\mathcal{F}
under the probability measure
\mathds{P}
.
Further, we write
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\,\cdot\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\,\cdot}f}}\right.}}}\right]
for conditional expectations given
\sigma(f)
, the
\sigma
-field generated by
f
, and
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\,\cdot\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\,\cdot}f,g}}\right.}}}\right]
for conditional expectations given
\sigma(\sigma(f)\cup\sigma(g))
.
Finally, for an event
A\in\mathcal{A}
with
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{A}}}\right]>0
and a sub-
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, we write
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{f}A}}\right.}}}\right]
for the expectation of
f
under
\mathds{P}_{A}
and
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{f}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]
for the conditional expectation of
f
given
\mathcal{F}
under the measure
\mathds{P}_{A}
.

The following two statements allow us to relate conditional expectations under different probability distributions.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathds{P})
be a probability space,
X
be an integrable random variable, let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be a sub-
\sigma
-field and let
A\in\mathcal{A}
with
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{A}}}\right]>0
.
Then

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}X}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]

\mathds{P}
-almost surely.
Moreover, it holds that

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]=\frac{\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}X}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]}{\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]}

\mathds{P}_{A}
-almost surely.

Proof.

By definition,
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.
Moreover, for any
B\in\mathcal{F}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{1}_{A}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}}}}\right]\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]}A}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}}}}\right]\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{B}X}A}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{1}_{A}X}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}X}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]}}}\right].

Thus, it holds that

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}X}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]

\mathds{P}
-almost surely.
Next, observe that for
A\in\mathcal{A}
with
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{A}}}\right]>0
it holds that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{A\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{A}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]>0

\mathds{P}_{A}
-almost surely.
Thus, it follows immediately that

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]=\frac{\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}X}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]}{\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]}

\mathds{P}_{A}
-almost surely.
∎

The following properties can be concluded.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathds{P})
be a probability space,
X
be an integrable random variable and
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be a sub-
\sigma
-field.
Let
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
be disjoint and such that
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{A_{i}}}}\right]>0
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}}}}\right]=1
.
If an
\mathcal{F}
-measurable function
f:\Omega\to\mathds{R}
is a version of
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A_{i}}}\right.}}}\right]
for all
1\leq i\leq n
, then
f
is also a version of
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]
.

Proof.

Since the events
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
and satisfy
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}}}}\right]=1
, we have

X=X\mathds{1}_{\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X\mathds{1}_{A_{i}}

\mathds{P}
-almost surely.
Thus, by linearity of expectation, we have

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\mathds{1}_{A_{i}}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X\mathds{1}_{A_{i}}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right].

Furthermore, since
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{A_{i}}}}\right]>0
for all
1\leq i\leq n

Lemma

A.1
gives

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\mathds{1}_{A_{i}}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X\mathds{1}_{A_{i}}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A_{i}}}\right.}}}\right]\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A_{i}}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A_{i}}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=f\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A_{i}}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A_{i}}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]

\mathds{P}
-almost surely.
Finally, observing that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A_{i}}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A_{i}}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=1

concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathds{P})
be a probability space,
X
be an integrable random variable, let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be a sub-
\sigma
-field and let
A\in\mathcal{A}
with
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{A}}}\right]>0
.

1.

If
A\in\mathcal{F}
then
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]

\mathds{P}_{A}
-almost surely.

2.

If
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}}\right.}}}\right]
for a
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
with
\sigma(X)\subseteq\mathcal{G}
, then
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]

\mathds{P}_{A}
-almost surely.

Proof.

For
1
, note that for
A\in\mathcal{F}
we have
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{1}_{A}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]
.
Moreover,
Lemma

A.1
yields

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{1}_{A}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right].

Thus, we have
\mathds{1}_{A}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{1}_{A}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]
and, in particular,
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F};A}}\right.}}}\right]

\mathds{P}_{A}
-almost surely.

For
2
, observe that

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}}\right.}}}\right]\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}}\right.}}}\right]}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}}\right.}}}\right]\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}}\right.}}}\right]}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]

\mathds{P}
-almost surely, where the second equality follows from
\sigma(X)\subseteq\mathcal{G}
and the third follows from
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{A}}\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}}}\right.}}}\right]
.
The claim now follows from
Lemma

A.1
.

∎

A.2
Regular conditional distributions

Consider a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathds{P})
with a sub-
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, a measure space
(D,\mathcal{D})
and
(D,\mathcal{D})
-valued random variable
X
.
A map
Q:\Omega\times\mathcal{D}\to[0,1]
is called a regular conditional distribution of
X
given
\mathcal{F}
if

1.

Q(\omega,\cdot)
is a probability measure on
(D,\mathcal{D})
for all
\omega\in\Omega
and

2.

Q(\cdot,A)
is a version of
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{X\in A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{X\in A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]
for all
A\in\mathcal{D}
.

The following statements makes regular conditional distributions particularly useful.

Theorem A.4

(
[
11
]
[Theorem
2.19
])
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathds{P})
be a probability space, let
X
be a
(D,\mathcal{D})
-valued random variable and let
Y
be a
(E,\mathcal{E})
-valued random variable.
If
Q:\Omega\times\mathcal{D}
is a regular conditional distribution of
X
given
\sigma(Y)
, then, for all
\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{E}
-measurable
f:D\times E\to\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
it holds that

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f(X,Y)\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{f(X,Y)}Y}}\right.}}}\right](\omega)=\int_{D}f(x,Y(\omega))Q(\omega,\,\text{d}x)

for
\mathds{P}
-almost all
\omega\in\Omega
.

Moreover, the following lemma helps to identify regular conditional distributions based on a
\pi
-system.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathds{P})
be a probability space and let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be a sub-
\sigma
-field.
Let
X
be a
(D,\mathcal{D})
-valued random variable on
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathds{P})
and let
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
be a
\pi
-system that generates
\mathcal{D}
.
Assume there is a function
Q:\Omega\times\mathcal{D}
such that

1.

Q(\omega,\cdot)
is a probability distribution on
(D,\mathcal{D})
for all
\omega\in\Omega
and

2.

Q(\cdot,G)
is a version of
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{X\in G}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{X\in G}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]
for all
G\in\mathcal{G}
.

Then
Q(\cdot,A)
is a version of
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{X\in A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{X\in A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]
for all
A\in\mathcal{D}
and, in particular,
Q
is a regular conditional distribution for
X
given
\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

Consider the set of events

\mathcal{H}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{A\in\mathcal{D}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{A\in\mathcal{D}}Q(\cdot,A)\text{ is a version of }\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{X\in A}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{X\in A}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]}}\right.}}\right\}.

Our goal is to prove
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{D}
.
To this end, note that
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
.
Thus, if we prove that
\mathcal{H}
is a Dynkin system, then the
\pi
-
\lambda
Theorem implies that
\mathcal{D}=\sigma(\mathcal{G})\subseteq\mathcal{H}
, which proves our claim.
To show that
\mathcal{H}
is a Dynkin system, we need to argue that
D\in\mathcal{H}
and that
\mathcal{H}
is closed under complements and countable disjoint unions.

To see that
D\in\mathcal{H}
, note that
Q(\omega,D)=1
for all
\omega\in\Omega
.
Thus,
Q(\cdot,\Omega)
is trivially
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.
Moreover, for any
B\in\mathcal{F}
, it holds that

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}Q(\cdot,D)\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{B}Q(\cdot,D)}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{B}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{1}_{X\in D}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{1}_{X\in D}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right],

which shows that
Q(\cdot,D)
is indeed a version of
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{X\in D}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{X\in D}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]
.

Next, fix some
G\in\mathcal{H}
and observe that
Q(\omega,(G)^{\mathrm{c}})=1-Q(\omega,G)
for all
\omega\in\Omega
.
Since
Q(\cdot,G)
is by assumption a version of
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{X\in G}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{X\in G}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]
(therefore
\mathcal{F}
-measurable), this shows that
