Q(\cdot,(G)^{\mathrm{c}})
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.
Moreover, for all
B\in\mathcal{F}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}Q(\cdot,(G)^{\mathrm{c}})\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{B}Q(\cdot,(G)^{\mathrm{c}})}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{B}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]-\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}Q(\cdot,G)\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{B}Q(\cdot,G)}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{1}_{X\in D}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{1}_{X\in D}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]-\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{1}_{X\in G}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{1}_{X\in G}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{1}_{X\in(G)^{\mathrm{c}}}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{1}_{X\in(G)^{\mathrm{c}}}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right],

which proves that
(G)^{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{H}
.

Finally, consider some sequence of disjoint events
(G_{n})_{n\in\mathds{N}}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mathds{N}}
and set
G=\bigcup_{n\in\mathds{N}}G_{n}
.
Note that
Q(\omega,G)=\sum_{n\in\mathds{N}}Q(\omega,G_{n})
for all
\omega\in\Omega
.
Since each function
Q(\cdot,G_{n})
is a version of
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{X\in G_{n}}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{X\in G_{n}}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]
, this implies that
Q(\cdot,G)
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.
Moreover, for all
B\in\mathcal{F}
it holds that

\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}Q(\cdot,G)\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{B}Q(\cdot,G)}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\sum_{n\in\mathds{N}}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}Q(\cdot,G_{n})\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{B}Q(\cdot,G_{n})}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\sum_{n\in\mathds{N}}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{1}_{X\in G_{n}}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{1}_{X\in G_{n}}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{1}_{X\in G}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{B}\mathds{1}_{X\in G}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right],

showing that
G\in\mathcal{H}
.
Thus,
\mathcal{H}
is a Dynkin system, which concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Hitting times and tail bounds

We frequently make use of the following version of Wald’s identity.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathds{P})
be a probability space, let
(X_{n})_{n\in N_{\geq 1}}
be a sequence of random variables on
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathds{P})
with values in
\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
and let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be a sub-
\sigma
-field.
Suppose there is a
\mathcal{F}
-measurable random variable
M
such that for all
n\in\mathds{N}
it holds that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{n}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{n}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]\leq M
almost surely.
Let
N
be a random variable in
\mathds{N}
such that for all
n\in\mathds{N}
it holds that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{n}\mathds{1}_{N\geq n}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{n}\mathds{1}_{N\geq n}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{n}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{n}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{N\geq n}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{N\geq n}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]
almost surely.
Then
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\sum_{n=1}^{N}X_{n}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\sum_{n=1}^{N}X_{n}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]\leq M\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{N\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{N}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]
almost surely.

Proof.

Using monotone convergence we have

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\sum_{n=1}^{N}X_{n}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\sum_{n=1}^{N}X_{n}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{N\geq n}X_{n}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{N\geq n}X_{n}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{n}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{X_{n}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathds{1}_{N\geq n}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\mathds{1}_{N\geq n}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq M\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{N\geq n}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{N\geq n}}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]=M\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{N\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{N}\mathcal{F}}}\right.}}}\right]\qed

Moreover, we use the following drift theorem to bound the expected number of iterations of our sampling algorithm.

Theorem B.2

(
[
48
, Theorem 1]
,
[
47
, Theorem 2]
)
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}}
be an integrable random process over
\mathds{R}
that is adapted to a filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}}
and let
T=\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{t\in\mathds{N}_{0}\mid X_{t}\leq 0}}\right\}
.
Assume

1.

X_{t}\mathds{1}_{T\geq t}\geq 0
for all
t\in N
and

2.

there is some
\varepsilon\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
such that
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{(X_{t}-X_{t+1})\mathds{1}_{T>t}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\ \vphantom{(X_{t}-X_{t+1})\mathds{1}_{T>t}}\mathcal{F}_{t}}}\right.}}}\right]\geq\varepsilon\mathds{1}_{T>t}
for all
t\in N
.

Then
\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T}}}\right]\leq\frac{\mathds{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X_{0}}}}\right]}{\varepsilon}
.
Further, suppose that

3.

X_{0}\leq x
for some
x\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
and

4.

there is some
c\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
such that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{t}-X_{t+1}}}\right|<c
for all
t\in\mathds{N}_{0}
.

Then, for all
s\geq\frac{2x}{\varepsilon}
,
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{T\geq s}}}\right]\leq\mathrm{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({-\frac{s\varepsilon^{2}}{16c^{2}}}}}\right)
.

Finally, we make use of the following tail bound for Poisson random variables.

Theorem B.3

(
[
59
, Theorem 5.4]
)
.

Let
Y\sim Pois(\rho)
for some
\rho\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
For all
y>\rho
it holds that
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{Y\geq y}}}\right]\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}\rho}{y}}}\right)^{y}
.

In particular, we use the following corollary of the above bound.

Corollary B.4
.

Let
Y\sim Pois(\rho)
for some
\rho\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
For all
\gamma>1
all
y\geq\mathrm{e}^{\gamma}\rho
it holds that
\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{Y\geq y}}}\right]\leq\mathrm{e}^{-(\gamma-1)y}
.

Proof.

Since
y\geq\mathrm{e}^{\gamma}\rho>\rho
,
Theorem

B.3
implies that

\mathds{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{Y\geq y}}}\right]\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}\rho}{y}}}\right)^{y}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}\rho}{\mathrm{e}^{\gamma}\rho}}}\right)^{y}=\mathrm{e}^{-(\gamma-1)y}.\qed

Appendix C
Gibbs point processes

Here we collect some useful lemmas about Gibbs point processes.

The following technical lemma will come in handy.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
\Lambda\in\mathcal{B}
and let
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\subseteq\Lambda
be a partitioning of
\Lambda
into measurable sets.
Let
\mathcal{G}
denote all events of the form
\{\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}\mid\eta\cap\Lambda_{1}\in A_{1},\eta\cap\Lambda_{2}\in A_{2}\}
with
A_{1}\in\mathfrak{R}_{\Lambda_{1}}
and
A_{2}\in\mathfrak{R}_{\Lambda_{2}}
.
Then
\mathcal{G}
is a
\pi
-system that satisfies
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}_{\Lambda}\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{G})
.

Proof.

Seeing that
\mathcal{G}
is a
\pi
-system is trivial.
For showing that
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}_{\Lambda}
, it suffices to show that for
i\in\{1,2\}
the projection
\pi_{\Lambda_{i}}:\eta\mapsto\eta\cap\Lambda_{i}
, viewed as a map
\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{i}}
, is
\mathfrak{R}
-
\mathfrak{R}_{\Lambda_{i}}
-measurable.
To this end, note that for every
k\in\mathds{N}_{0}\cup\{\infty\}
and
\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda_{i}
the event
E=\{\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{i}}\mid\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta\cap\Lambda^{\prime}}}\right|=k\}\in\mathfrak{R}_{\Lambda_{i}}
satisfies

\pi_{\Lambda_{1}}^{-1}(E)=\{\eta\in\mathcal{N}\mid\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta\cap\Lambda^{\prime}}}\right|=k\}\in\mathfrak{R}.

As such events
E
generate
\mathfrak{R}_{\Lambda_{i}}
, the desired measurability of
\pi_{\Lambda_{i}}
follows, which proves the inclusion
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}_{\Lambda}
.

For
\mathfrak{R}_{\Lambda}\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{G})
, let
E=\{\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}\mid\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta\cap\Lambda^{\prime}}}\right|=k\}\in\mathfrak{R}_{\Lambda}
for some
k\in\mathds{N}_{0}\cup\{\infty\}
and
\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda_{i}
, and observe that such events generate
\mathfrak{R}_{\Lambda}
.
Next, observe that such an event
E
can be expressed as

E=\bigcup_{k_{1}+k_{2}=k}\{\eta\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}\mid\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta\cap\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda^{\prime}}}\right|=k_{1},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta\cap\Lambda_{2}\cap\Lambda^{\prime}}}\right|=k_{2}\}.

As, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, it holds that
\{\eta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{i}}\mid\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta^{\prime}\cap(\Lambda_{i}\cap\Lambda^{\prime})}}\right|=k_{i}\}\in\mathfrak{R}_{\Lambda_{i}}
, we have thus written
E
as a countable union of element of
\mathcal{G}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

In this bounded-range setting, the following lemma might be seen as a version of the spatial Markov property for partition functions.

Lemma C.2
.

Let
\phi
be a repulsive potential with bounded range
r\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Moreover, let
{\Lambda^{\prime}}\subseteq\Lambda
and let be any activity function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}:\mathds{R}^{d}\to\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
.
For every two point configurations
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
with
\mathrm{\mathrm{dist}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\Lambda^{\prime}},\eta_{1}\ominus\eta_{2}}}\right)\geq r
, where
\ominus
denotes the symmetric difference, it holds that
Z_{{\Lambda^{\prime}}}^{\eta_{1}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=Z_{{\Lambda^{\prime}}}^{\eta_{2}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
.

Proof.

Since the range of
\phi
is bounded by
r
, it holds that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\eta_{1}}(x)=\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\eta_{2}}(x)
for all
x\in\Lambda
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle Z_{{\Lambda^{\prime}}}^{\eta_{1}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{k!}\int_{{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{k}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\eta_{1}}^{\boldsymbol{x}}\mathrm{e}^{-H\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{x}}}\right)}\,\text{d}\boldsymbol{x}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{k!}\int_{{\Lambda^{\prime}}^{k}}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\eta_{2}}^{\boldsymbol{x}}\mathrm{e}^{-H\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{x}}}\right)}\,\text{d}\boldsymbol{x}=Z_{{\Lambda^{\prime}}}^{\eta_{2}}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}),

which proves the claim.
∎
